Low-Dose Tomography of Random Fields and the Problem of Continuous Heterogeneity
Ho Yun, Alessia Caponera, Victor M. Panaretosabel=e2, mark]victor.panaretos@epfl.ch

Abstract
We consider the problem of nonparametric estimation of the conformational variability in a population of related structures, based on low-dose tomography of a random sample of representative individuals. In this context, each individual represents a random perturbation of a common template and is imaged noisily and discretely at but a few projection angles. Such problems arise in the cryo Electron Microscopy of structurally heterogeneous biological macromolecules. We model the population as a random field, whose mean captures the typical structure, and whose covariance reflects the heterogeneity. We show that consistent estimation is achievable with as few as two projections per individual, and derive uniform convergence rates reflecting how the various parameters of the problem affect statistical efficiency, and their trade-offs. Our analysis formulates the domain of the forward operator to be a reproducing kernel Hilbert space, where we establish representer and Mercer theorems tailored to question at hand. This allows us to exploit pooling estimation strategies central to functional data analysis, illustrating their versatility in a novel context. We provide an efficient computational implementation using tensorized Krylov methods and demonstrate the performance of our methodology by way of simulation.

1
Introduction

In the classical problem of tomography
[
70
,
38
,
61
,
13
]
, one is interested in reconstructing a fixed three-dimensional structure, given two-dimensional projections thereof at several different orientations. The fixed structure is represented by a probability density function in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, and its projections are taken to be two-dimensional marginal distributions, obtained by integrating along the viewing direction. Statistical versions of this problem arise when randomness is introduced “into the equation”
[
117
,
114
,
49
]
. Classically, this happens through measurement error or random sampling: one observes pixelated and noise-corrupted projections at a finite number of randomly sampled orientations.

Yet, randomness can also be inherent at the level of the three-dimensional structure itself: when the structure is not fixed, but can manifest in various different but related configurations
[
1
,
90
,
98
]
. In this case, the target is not a single fixed structure, but rather a population of related structures that can be conceptualized as perturbations of some common template. The estimand, therefore, is not a three-dimensional density but rather the law of a three-dimensional random field, making the problem intrinsically statistical at the signal level
[
93
,
26
]
. Correspondingly, the tomographic data are characterized by heterogeneity, as amongst the two dimensional projections appear the marginals of various
different
instantiations of the three dimensional structure – observed still at random orientations with noise contamination.

This heterogeneous tomography problem arises perhaps most prominently in single-particle cryogenic Electron Microscopy (cryo-EM), a Nobel-prize winning imaging modality for determining the three-dimensional electron density structure of biological macromolecules
[
24
,
55
,
73
,
12
,
3
]
. In cryo-EM, thousands of macromolecules of identical composition are imaged under a microscope after being vitrified at randomly assumed orientations
[
20
,
101
]
. If an identical composition always yielded an identical potential density, this would reduce to a standard “homogeneous” tomographic problem. However, macromolecules typically exist in varying configurations due to their flexibility and/or their biological functioning. And, increasingly, these configurations are viewed not as a few discrete states but as a continuum characterized by an energy landscape
[
25
,
26
]
. The challenge is thus to retrieve not a single structure, but rather the probability law of a continuum random field – the law encoding the said conformational landscape. For biophysicists, this challenge is also a great opportunity, as the ability to experimentally determine the conformational distribution of a biological macromolecule can lead to more systematic functional interpretations.

A concurrent challenge in heterogeneous cryo-EM is the
low-dose
problem
[
30
,
101
]
: if the conformational landscape is continuous, each manifestation essentially occurs only once in the sample, and so we can have but a few vantage points thereof, limited to the number of tilts taken for imaging. Ideally, one would collect many tilts per particle to gather more data, but exposing biological samples to a high electron dose destroys their structure
[
37
,
31
]
. One is thus often limited to a low-dose setting, where each realization of the random field is imaged with a minimally sufficient number of tilt angles
[
4
,
77
]
.

Motivated by cryo-EM, this paper aims to develop the statistical foundations related to the problem of (continuously) heterogeneous tomography in a possibly low-dose setting. This includes theory and rates, as well as methodology and implementation. To do so, we formulate the heterogeneity problem as one of inferring the modes of variation of the three-dimensional random object (random field) around a template structure, as one would do in a functional principal component analysis (PCA).
This effectively reduces the problem to that of non-parametrically estimating the random field’s mean structure (the template) and the covariance kernel (which encodes the modes of variation around the template).
Though functional PCA is arguably a coarse descriptor of heterogeneity,
it provides a framework that is amenable to theoretical analysis, while retaining enough of the key elements of reality to draw useful, if approximate, conclusions. Indeed, this framework allows us to fruitfully interface with the field of Functional Data Analysis (FDA)
[
42
,
85
]
, where the non-parametric estimation of means and covariances of random processes from sparse and noisy data is a central and well-understood problem
[
76
,
29
,
9
,
10
,
8
,
7
]
– albeit not yet in the complex setting of tomography. It also allows us to develop a rigorous statistical analysis of the problem where the unknowns are in the continuum, but the data are recorded discretely and with measurement error. Our analysis shows the interplay between smoothness conditions and experimental parameters, such as the number of instantiations imaged, and the number of vantage points (tilts) per instantiation, including related phase transitions. Interestingly, it is shown that consistent recovery of both mean (template) and covariance (heterogeneity) is feasible with as few as two vantage points per instantiation, provided the number of instantiations diverges. Parallel to our theoretical results, we also provide complete methodology, including a computational implementation. Our methodology leverages reproducing kernel Hilbert spaces, by establishing bespoke Mercer and representer theorems. Our implementation uses tensorized Krylov methods
[
118
]
to yield computationally
efficient approach, despite the dimensionality of the estimands.

2
Background and Contributions

Given an integrable
f:\mathbb{B}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
on the unit ball, the X-ray transform
f\mapsto\{\mathscr{P}_{R}f:R\in SO(d)\}
generates its collection of
marginals
along the axis
\boldsymbol{r}:=R^{\top}e_{d}
of rotation
R
from the special orthogonal group
SO(d)

[
13
,
102
]
.
More generally, the X-ray transform is a special case of the
k
-plane transform, which integrates
f
over
d_{r}
-dimensional planes
[
103
,
104
,
97
]
:

\displaystyle\mathscr{P}_{R}f(\boldsymbol{x})\equiv\mathscr{P}f(R,\boldsymbol{x}):=\int_{(1-\|\boldsymbol{x}\|^{2})^{1/2}\cdot\mathbb{B}^{d_{r}}}f(R^{-1}[\boldsymbol{x}:z])\mathrm{d}z,\quad\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathbb{B}^{d-d_{r}}.

(1)

where
[\boldsymbol{x}:z]
represents the vector obtained by concatenating
\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d-d_{r}}
and
z\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{r}}
. Different values of
d_{r}
correspond to different imaging modalities, such as the X-ray transform (
d_{r}=1
) for CT and cryo-EM, the Radon transform (
d_{r}=d-1
)
[
70
]
, or nuclear magnetic resonance imaging (
d_{r}=d-2
)
[
78
]
.

The idealized
homogeneous
tomographic reconstruction consists of recovering
f
from the complete collection of its projections,
R\mapsto\mathscr{P}_{R}f
, known as the
sinogram

[
70
,
38
,
61
,
13
]
. In practice, however, one only has access to a finite number of “sinogram values”
\{\mathscr{P}_{\boldsymbol{R}_{j}}f\}_{j=1}^{r}
, each evaluated at discrete locations
\boldsymbol{X}_{jk}\in\mathbb{B}^{d-1}
and possibly contaminated by noise:

Z_{jk}=\mathscr{P}_{\boldsymbol{R}_{j}}f(\boldsymbol{X}_{jk})+\varepsilon_{jk}.

In statistical settings, the rotations
\boldsymbol{R}_{j}
, locations
\boldsymbol{X}_{jk}
, and measurement errors
\varepsilon_{jk}
will be random and completely independent. We refer to this setting as
homogeneous
tomography, because it is always the same object
f
that is imaged repeatedly, just from different angles. Incidentally, the term
sinogram
arises from the sinusoidal patterns that off-center structures create in the projection data, as seen in
Fig.

1
. Accordingly, sinograms must satisfy certain moment constraints, known as the Helgason-Ludwig consistency conditions
[
61
,
38
]
.

Fig 1:
Left
: The true mean function.
Center
: Its ideal sinogram over 180
∘
.
Right
: Merged and noisy discrete sinogram from a simulation with
n=100
random functions, each observed at only
r=2
random angles and
s=30
locations, where different color corresponds to the observations from a different function.

To formulate the heterogeneous problem
[
1
,
90
,
51
,
98
]
, we model the object of interest as a second-order random field
\mathcal{Y}
on the unit ball
\mathbb{B}^{d}
. The law of
\mathcal{Y}
captures the continuous structural heterogeneity of the object, and we aim to estimate its mean
\boldsymbol{\mu}
and covariance
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}:=\mathbb{E}\mathcal{Y},\quad\boldsymbol{\Gamma}:=\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{Y}\otimes\mathcal{Y}],\quad\boldsymbol{\Sigma}:=\mathbb{E}[(\mathcal{Y}-\boldsymbol{\mu})\otimes(\mathcal{Y}-\boldsymbol{\mu})]=\boldsymbol{\Gamma}-\boldsymbol{\mu}\otimes\boldsymbol{\mu},

(2)

in order to perform PCA that reveals the main modes of conformational variation around its mean. We assume access only to low-dose tomographic measurements on i.i.d. replicates:

Z_{ijk}=\mathscr{P}_{\boldsymbol{R}_{ij}}\mathcal{Y}_{i}(\boldsymbol{X}_{ijk})+\varepsilon_{ijk},\quad 1\leq i\leq n,\,1\leq j\leq r_{i},\,1\leq k\leq s_{ij},

where:

•

each replication
\mathcal{Y}_{i}
is imaged at replication-specific orientations or
tilts

\boldsymbol{R}_{ij}
.

•

each projection image
\mathscr{P}_{\boldsymbol{R}_{ij}}\mathcal{Y}_{i}
is measured at its own evaluation points
\boldsymbol{X}_{ijk}
.

•

the errors
\varepsilon_{ijk}
are independent, zero-mean, with variance
\sigma^{2}_{r_{i}}
that can depend on
r_{i}
.

•

the viewing angles
\boldsymbol{R}_{ij}
and evaluation points
\boldsymbol{X}_{ijk}
are drawn at random, independently of each other and of the measurement errors.

To incorporate the
low-dose
aspect, we allow the noise variance
\sigma^{2}_{r_{i}}
to depend on the number of tilts
r_{i}
. Concretely, we think of
r\mapsto\sigma^{2}_{r}
as an increasing function of
r
(e.g.
\sigma^{2}_{r}\propto r
), reflecting the fact that that there is a
radiation dose restriction
: the total radiation per individual should be limited, so that there is a trade-off between the number of viewing angles and the noise contamination of the corresponding images. In other words, an individual’s radiation dose is pre-set, and this fixed budget must be distributed among the
r_{i}
viewing angles; more angles result in a lower dose and higher noise for each projection image.

For estimation, we assume that the fields
\mathcal{Y}_{i}
are random elements in a Reproducing Kernel Hilbert Space (RKHS)
\mathbb{H}(K)

[
2
,
79
]
. Though there are other possible smoothness conditions
[
107
,
106
,
65
,
50
,
69
,
97
,
96
]
to mitigate the inherent ill-posedness, the RKHS setting is particularly well-suited to our context: by deriving bespoke Mercer and representer theorems, we are able to reduce the problem to a ridge regression problem via feature maps
\{\varphi(\boldsymbol{R}_{ij},\boldsymbol{X}_{ijk}):1\leq i\leq n,1\leq j\leq r_{i},1\leq k\leq s_{ij}\}
adapted to the observation scheme, leading to less pronounced artifacts
[
117
]
. Crucially, this allows the recovery of both the mean and covariance, as the covariance problem also admits a representer theorem using a dictionary of tensor products of the feature maps used for the mean. This opens the way for using pooling techniques common in FDA
[
116
,
105
]
. One might suspect that the Gram matrices required for implementing such a strategy are prohibitively large to store or manipulate directly. This is true, and we employ a tensorized Krylov method that bypasses their explicit construction
[
118
]
. After solving for the coefficient matrix, a generalized eigendecomposition of this matrix yields the sought functional PCA
of
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{\nu,\eta}=\hat{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}_{\eta}-\hat{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{\nu}\otimes\hat{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{\nu}
, which represents the main modes of continuous heterogeneity.

We then derive uniform convergence rates for both the mean and covariance estimators under a source condition on the distribution of
\mathcal{Y}_{i}
’s. Our results generalize known convergence rates from the standard FDA setting (where
\mathscr{P}_{R}=I
)
[
6
,
7
,
9
,
8
]
to this considerably more complex setting. Furthermore, our findings reveal several crucial phase transitions that are governed by the number of viewing angles and the low-dose imaging constraint:

•

We establish the minimum tilts required for consistent estimation as the number of particles
n
grows. For the mean, as few as one tilt per particle is sufficient. As for the covariance, consistent estimation is possible with just two tilts per particle. Then, the convergence rate depends on the number of tilts, not on the locational sampling frequency.

•

We identify a phase transition based on how the number of tilts compares to the number of particles
n
. In a dense-tilt regime, where the number of tilts is sufficiently large, the error is limited by
n
and we achieve a parametric rate of convergence.

•

We formally characterize the trade-off imposed by a fixed radiation budget. The convergence rate slows as the noise
\sigma_{r}^{2}
increases faster with the number of tilts
r
. For mean estimation, the rate actually degrades as more tilts are added, if the noise variance grows faster than linearly (
\sigma_{r}^{2}\propto r
). For covariance estimation, the boundary is much stricter: the rate degrades if noise variance grows faster than the square root of
r
(
\sigma_{r}^{2}\propto\sqrt{r}
).

The remainder of this paper is organized as follows.
Section

3
reviews related work on the heterogeneous tomography problem, and
Section

4
introduces key notation. We develop our main theoretical contributions in
Sections

5
,
6
and
7
, where we establish our RKHS framework, adapt Mercer’s theory for the inverse problem setting, and derive the asymptotic properties of our estimators. We note that our proofs rely on the abstract property of orientational duality, making our results applicable to general
k
-plane transforms (Supplement
A
).

Section

8
discusses how to apply our theory to the cryo-EM problem with unknown orientations, and
Section

9
presents simulation studies.

3
Related Work

The numerous methods developed to address heterogeneity analysis in single-particle cryo-EM can be broadly classified into two paradigms. A common prerequisite for these methods is the prior estimation of particle viewing angles, typically achieved through 3D alignment based on the Fourier slice theorem
[
81
,
70
]
.

The first paradigm,
discrete
heterogeneity, assumes that biomolecules exist in a finite number of stable states and frames the problem as a 3D classification task, where the primary goal is to sort particle images into distinct classes and reconstruct a high-resolution 3D map for each. Early methods like multi-body refinement treated molecules as collections of rigid parts
[
41
]
. This evolved into the unsupervised classification methods, including two highly successful implementations
RELION

[
91
,
90
]
and
cryoSPARC

[
84
]
, both of which leverage regularized maximum-likelihood approaches. For numerical optimization, they use expectation-maximization or stochastic gradient descent, respectively. However, these methods tend to struggle when the number of conformations is large or the variations are subtle.

To overcome these limitations, methods that model
continuous
heterogeneity have emerged as an alternative, see
[
1
,
51
]
and the references therein. These techniques aim to characterize the primary modes of molecular transition within a lower-dimensional space. The main approaches include:

•

Linear Subspace Methods
: This approach
[
82
,
81
,
80
,
110
,
51
,
1
,
28
]
assumes that
even complex molecular motions can be approximated by a few simple, linear components. The primary approach involves estimating the full mean and covariance of the molecular ensemble, then performing PCA, a methodology that shares a foundation with our paper.

•

Manifold Learning Methods
: Rather than assuming motions are linear, these methods posit they follow curved paths or surfaces. They embed the high-dimensional cryo-EM data into a latent space, using kernel functions
[
26
,
111
]
or graph Laplacian
[
66
]
, and performing PCA thereof for dimension reduction.

•

Physics-Inspired Methods
:
This line of work models structural changes using more direct physical analogies, including methods that fit rigid bodies into a flexible assembly
[
68
]
, calculate smooth warping fields to morph a consensus structure
[
83
,
92
]
, or incorporate constraints from molecular dynamics
[
39
]
.

•

Deep Learning Methods
: These methods leverage neural networks to learn complex, non-linear latent representations of the conformational landscape from the data
[
121
,
11
]
.

Common to these methods, however, is the
discretization
of the structure into a cubic voxel grid, which is used to estimate the covariance of the 3D volumes that often requires data downsampling to remain numerically tractable
[
60
,
59
]
. Furthermore, most of these methods depend on the Filtered Back-Projection (FBP), and with the exception of PCA-based approaches, often lack formal performance guarantees but rely on numerical evidence
[
94
]
.

Our work, therefore, takes a conceptual step beyond the aforementioned literature
– we perceive the underlying biomolecular structure as a continuous random function, approaching the problem from a
functional
viewpoint. Instead of relying on a voxel grid, we encode the smoothness of the conformational space using a RKHS, just as the Nyquist-Shannon sampling frequency
[
95
]
dictates the resolution limit of reconstruction. This perspective leads to a reconstruction scheme based on kernel regression that is fundamentally different from FBP and is, crucially, dimension-agnostic – it does not suffer from FBP’s inconsistent behavior in odd versus even dimensions due to the involvement of the Hilbert transform, a global operation
[
70
]
. By abstracting away from the fine experimental details (e.g., contrast transfer function or translation alignment), our simplified model, much like foundational
stylized models
in statistics like the spiked covariance or stochastic block models, is intended to provide an analytically tractable setting
providing theoretical insight, despite being a coarse approximation.

4
Notation

Throughout the paper, we adhere to the following notation:

•

\mathbb{S}^{d-1}=\{\boldsymbol{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\|\boldsymbol{z}\|=1\}
is the unit sphere in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|=\frac{2\pi^{d/2}}{\Gamma(d/2)},\quad|\mathbb{S}^{0}|=2,\,|\mathbb{S}^{1}|=2\pi,\,|\mathbb{S}^{2}|=4\pi.

•

\mathbb{B}^{d}=\{\boldsymbol{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\|\boldsymbol{z}\|\leq 1\}
is the closed unit ball in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

•

For a given linear operator
\mathscr{P}
, the null and range spaces are denoted by
\mathcal{N}(\mathscr{P})
and
\mathcal{R}(\mathscr{P})
, respectively.
†
symbol denotes the Moore-Penrose generalized inverse.

We frequently use tensor products and recall key facts. To make the abstract tensor algebra concrete, it is customary in the FDA literature
[
42
]
to define the elementary tensor
f_{1}\otimes f_{2}
, for
f_{1}\in\mathcal{H}_{1}
,
f_{2}\in\mathcal{H}_{2}
in separable
\mathbb{R}
-Hilbert spaces, as

f_{1}\otimes f_{2}\,:\,f^{\prime}_{2}\in\mathcal{H}_{2}\quad\mapsto\quad\langle f_{2},f^{\prime}_{2}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{2}}\,f_{1}\in\mathcal{H}_{1}.

In our covariance analysis, we encounter fourth-order tensors
f_{1}\otimes f_{2}\otimes f_{3}\otimes f_{4}
, and the grouping of these terms matters:

•

Thinking of operators acting on operators, the natural grouping is
(f_{1}\otimes f_{2})\otimes(f_{3}\otimes f_{4})
.

•

For spectral analysis, it is more natural to group the vectors and covectors separately
[
57
,
34
]
. This corresponds to a
reshuffling
of the tensor into
(f_{1}\otimes f_{3})
and
(f_{2}\otimes f_{4})
. To distinguish this second case, we use the special notation
(f_{1}\otimes f_{3})\otimes_{2}(f_{2}\otimes f_{4})
.

The tensor product space
\mathcal{H}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{2}
is formally the metric completion of the vector space spanned by these elementary tensors, and the universal property
[
47
]
reveals that
\mathcal{H}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{2}
is isometric and linearly isomorphic to the space of Hilbert-Schmidt (HS) operators
\mathcal{B}_{2}(\mathcal{H}_{2},\mathcal{H}_{1})
. This space is itself a separable Hilbert space with inner product

\langle\mathscr{K}_{1},\mathscr{K}_{2}\rangle_{HS}:=\sum_{l=1}^{\infty}\langle\mathscr{K}_{1}e_{l},\mathscr{K}_{2}e_{l}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{2}},

where
\{e_{l}\}_{l=1}^{\infty}
is any complete orthonormal system (CONS) for
\mathcal{H}_{1}
. Note that for elementary tensors, it holds that
\langle f_{1}\otimes f_{2},f^{\prime}_{1}\otimes f^{\prime}_{2}\rangle_{HS}=\langle f_{1},f^{\prime}_{1}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{1}}\langle f_{2},f^{\prime}_{2}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{2}}
.

We denote the Banach space of bounded operators by
\mathcal{B}_{\infty}(\mathcal{H})
, equipped with the operator norm
\VERT\cdot\VERT_{\infty}
. The Banach space of compact operators on
\mathcal{H}
is denoted by
(\mathcal{B}_{0}(\mathcal{H}),\VERT\cdot\VERT_{\infty})
. For
1\leq p<\infty
, the
p
-Schatten class on
\mathcal{H}
is a collection of operators as follows:

\mathcal{B}_{p}(\mathcal{H}):=\left\{\mathscr{K}\in\mathcal{B}_{0}(\mathcal{H}):\VERT\mathscr{K}\VERT_{p}:=\sum_{l=1}^{\infty}\langle e_{l},(\mathscr{K}^{*}\mathscr{K})^{p/2}e_{l}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}<+\infty\right\},

for any CONS
\{e_{l}\}_{l=1}^{\infty}
for
\mathcal{H}
. It can be shown that
(\mathcal{B}_{p}(\mathcal{H}),\VERT\cdot\VERT_{p})
is a Banach space, and the
p
-Schatten norm
\VERT\cdot\VERT_{p}
is called the trace and the HS norm when
p=1
and
p=2
, respectively.

5
Modeling

If the support of the
d
-dimensional function is compact, we can simply assume it to be the unit ball
\mathbb{B}^{d}
.
When the orientation
R
is also considered as an input in (
1
), we use the notation
\mathscr{P}f(R,\boldsymbol{x})=\mathscr{P}_{R}f(\boldsymbol{x})
interchangeably, and
\mathscr{P}
is referred to as the sinogram operator in this context.
Although we focus on the X-ray transform in the main body, the theoretical results presented in this paper are not limited to them. We treat the problem more abstractly, as detailed in Supplement
A
. For instance, the orientation group
\mathbb{G}=SO(d)
can be viewed as a compact Lie group, equipped with the normalized Haar measure
\mathrm{d}R

[
35
,
22
]
. The Lie group acts from the left on a compact domain
\Omega^{d}=\mathbb{B}^{d}
:
\rho(R):\Omega^{d}\rightarrow\Omega^{d},\boldsymbol{z}\mapsto R\boldsymbol{z}
. Moving forward, the domain for the resulting projection images is
\Omega^{d-d_{r}}=\mathbb{B}^{d-d_{r}}
, with
d_{r}=1
, and we use the same notation to denote the left group action on functions. To be specific, for
f:\Omega^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
and
g:\mathbb{G}\times\Omega^{d-d_{r}}\to\mathbb{R}
, we have:

\displaystyle\rho(R)f(\boldsymbol{z}):=f(R^{-1}\boldsymbol{z})

\displaystyle,\quad\boldsymbol{z}\in\Omega^{d},

(3)

\displaystyle\rho(R_{1})g(R_{2},\boldsymbol{x}):=g(R_{1}R_{2},\boldsymbol{x})

\displaystyle,\quad R_{1},R_{2}\in\mathbb{G},\,\boldsymbol{x}\in\Omega^{d-d_{r}}.

(4)

Rather than the specific integral formula in (
1
), the key property we distill from the X-ray transform, or the
k
-plane transform more generally, is
orientational duality
:
\mathscr{P}_{R}=\mathscr{P}_{I}\circ\rho(R)
for any
R\in\mathbb{G}
, which has a clear physical interpretation. In CT imaging, for instance, scanning an object at
30^{\circ}
is equivalent to rotating the object by
-30^{\circ}
and then scanning it at
0^{\circ}
. Because our proofs rely only on this property and some mild smoothness conditions (see
Definition

A.1
), our findings naturally extend to any operator that shares this structure.

Consider weight functions
W_{0,d}:\Omega^{d}\rightarrow[0,+\infty)
and
W_{d_{r},d}:\Omega^{d-d_{r}}\rightarrow[0,+\infty)
for input and output functions, which have finite total weight:

\omega_{0,d}:=\int_{\Omega^{d}}W_{0,d}(\boldsymbol{z})\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{z}<\infty,\quad\omega_{d_{r},d}:=\int_{\Omega^{d-d_{r}}}W_{d_{r},d}(\boldsymbol{x})\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}<\infty,

(5)

where
\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{z}
and
\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}
represent the standard Lebesgue measures. We assume that the input weight function
W_{0,d}
is
\mathbb{G}
-invariant, i.e.,
W_{0,d}(\boldsymbol{z})=W_{0,d}(R\boldsymbol{z})
for any
R\in\mathbb{G}
. Consequently, the input space
\mathcal{L}_{2}(\Omega^{d},W_{0,d})
is a well-defined Hilbert space containing
\mathcal{C}(\Omega^{d})
, with its inner product given by:

\langle f_{1},f_{2}\rangle_{W_{0,d}}=\int_{\Omega^{d}}f_{1}(\boldsymbol{z})f_{2}(\boldsymbol{z})W_{0,d}(\boldsymbol{z})\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{z}.

The same holds for the output space
\mathcal{L}_{2}(\Omega^{d-d_{r}},W_{d_{r},d})
:

\langle g_{1},g_{2}\rangle_{W_{d_{r},d}}=\int_{\Omega^{d-d_{r}}}g_{1}(\boldsymbol{x})g_{2}(\boldsymbol{x})W_{d_{r},d}(\boldsymbol{x})\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}.

Similarly,
\mathcal{L}_{2}(\mathbb{G}\times\Omega^{d-d_{r}},W_{d_{r},d})
forms a Hilbert space with the inner product defined as:

\displaystyle\langle g_{1},g_{2}\rangle_{W_{d_{r},d}}:=\int_{\mathbb{G}}\int_{\Omega^{d-d_{r}}}g_{1}(R,\boldsymbol{x})g_{2}(R,\boldsymbol{x})W_{d_{r},d}(\boldsymbol{x})\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\mathrm{d}R,

5.1
RKHS Framework

To assess reconstruction reliability, it is essential to equip the function space with a smoothness structure. In the literature on the X-ray and Radon transforms, Sobolev spaces
[
69
]
or their variants
[
69
,
97
,
96
]
have been explored as alternatives to the weighted
\mathcal{L}_{2}
domain. Building upon this,
[
117
]
generalized this concept by framing the function space as an RKHS. RKHSs encode desired levels of smoothness through its reproducing kernel, which is often constructed via Green’s functions
[
42
,
79
]
. The RKHS framework is particularly appealing due to its strong theoretical foundation and the representer theorem, which underpins its widespread use in statistics and machine learning
[
54
,
53
,
113
]
.

Definition 5.1
.

Let
\mathbb{H}
be a Hilbert space of real-valued functions defined on a set
\Omega
. A bivariate function
K:\Omega\times\Omega\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is called a reproducing kernel for
\mathbb{H}
if

1.

For any
\boldsymbol{z}\in\Omega
, a generator
k_{\boldsymbol{z}}(\cdot):=K(\cdot,\boldsymbol{z})
at
\boldsymbol{z}\in\Omega
belongs to
\mathbb{H}
.

2.

For any
f\in\mathbb{H}
, the point evaluation at
\boldsymbol{z}\in\Omega
is given by
f(\boldsymbol{z})=\langle f,k_{\boldsymbol{z}}\rangle_{\mathbb{H}}
.

A Hilbert space equipped with a reproducing kernel is called an RKHS.

The Moore-Aronszajn theorem
[
2
]
states that any reproducing kernel
K
is symmetric and semi-positive definite (s.p.d.), and conversely, any such a kernel uniquely defines an RKHS, denoted
\mathbb{H}=\mathbb{H}(K)

[
79
,
42
]
. When the kernel is continuous, it is referred to as a Mercer kernel. In our work, it is particularly relevant to
consider a
\mathbb{G}
-invariant Mercer kernel, meaning
K(\boldsymbol{z}_{1},\boldsymbol{z}_{2})=K(R\boldsymbol{z}_{1},R\boldsymbol{z}_{2})
for all
R\in\mathbb{G}
. We call this type of kernel as the sinogram kernel, see
Definition

A.5
for a general definition. For the X-ray transform, the evaluation functional of a projection image at any
\boldsymbol{x}\in\Omega^{d-d_{r}}
is bounded by some constant
B>0
:

|\mathscr{P}_{R}f(\boldsymbol{x})|\leq B\|f\|_{\mathbb{H}},\quad R\in\mathbb{G},\,f\in\mathbb{H}(K),

see
Remark

A.7
. Consequently, the Riesz representation theorem guarantees that there exists a unique feature map
\varphi(R,\boldsymbol{x})\in\mathbb{H}
with
\|\varphi(R,\boldsymbol{x})\|_{\mathbb{H}}\leq B
, yielding the reproducing property:

\langle\varphi(R,\boldsymbol{x}),f\rangle_{\mathbb{H}}=\mathscr{P}f(R,\boldsymbol{x}),\quad f\in\mathbb{H}(K).

(6)

The significant advantage of this framework is that we can explicitly construct the feature map using the reproducing property (
6
):
\varphi(R,\boldsymbol{x})(\boldsymbol{z})=\langle\varphi(R,\boldsymbol{x}),k_{\boldsymbol{z}}\rangle_{\mathbb{H}}=\mathscr{P}k_{\boldsymbol{z}}(R,\boldsymbol{x})
, which only requires the forward operator

\mathscr{P}
, not its inverse or adjoint.

5.2
Estimation

Let
\mathcal{Y}
be a second-order random function taking values in the RKHS
\mathbb{H}=\mathbb{H}(K)
, that is
\mathbb{E}\|\mathcal{Y}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}<\infty
. Let
\boldsymbol{\mu},\boldsymbol{\Gamma},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
denote the mean, second moment, and covariance of the random element
\mathcal{Y}
, respectively, as defined in (
2
).
Let
\mathbb{P}_{\boldsymbol{R}}
and
\mathbb{P}_{\boldsymbol{X}}
denote the probability measures on
\mathbb{G}
and
\Omega^{d-d_{r}}
, respectively, given by

\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}_{\boldsymbol{R}}(R)=\mathrm{d}R,\quad\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}_{\boldsymbol{X}}(\boldsymbol{x})=\omega_{d_{r},d}^{-1}W_{d_{r},d}(\boldsymbol{x})\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}.

We consider
n
i.i.d. copies
\{\mathcal{Y}_{i}:i=1,\dots,n\}
of
\mathcal{Y}
. For each sample
\mathcal{Y}_{i}
, we generate projections
\mathscr{P}_{\boldsymbol{R}_{ij}}\mathcal{Y}_{i}
at random orientations
\boldsymbol{R}_{ij}
. These projections are then observed at random locations
\boldsymbol{X}_{ijk}
under the noise
\varepsilon_{ijk}
:

Z_{ijk}=\mathscr{P}\mathcal{Y}_{i}(\boldsymbol{R}_{ij},\boldsymbol{X}_{ijk})+\varepsilon_{ijk}=\langle\mathcal{Y}_{i},\varphi(\boldsymbol{R}_{ij},\boldsymbol{X}_{ijk})\rangle_{\mathbb{H}}+\varepsilon_{ijk},

(7)

where
\boldsymbol{R}_{ij}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle iid}}{{\sim}}\mathbb{P}_{\boldsymbol{R}}
,

\boldsymbol{X}_{ijk}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle iid}}{{\sim}}\mathbb{P}_{\boldsymbol{X}}
, and
\varepsilon_{ijk}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle iid}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma_{r_{i}}^{2})
are all mutually independent, with
1\leq i\leq n,1\leq j\leq r_{i},1\leq k\leq s_{ij}
. For brevity, we denote
\varphi_{ijk}
instead of
\varphi(\boldsymbol{R}_{ij},\boldsymbol{X}_{ijk})
, an
\mathbb{H}
-valued random element with its norm bounded by
B>0
.

To estimate the mean and covariance, we employ Tikhonov regularization, which yields finite-dimensional ridge regressions in our framework, owing to the reproducing property (
6
), with the normal equation provided by suitable Gram matrices. To formulate this, we first derive the conditional expectation given the random orientations and locations:

\mathbb{E}[Z_{ijk}|(\boldsymbol{R}_{ij},\boldsymbol{X}_{ijk})]=\mathscr{P}\boldsymbol{\mu}(\boldsymbol{R}_{ij},\boldsymbol{X}_{ijk})=\langle\boldsymbol{\mu},\varphi_{ijk}\rangle_{\mathbb{H}}.

This leads to the empirical risk functional
\hat{L}_{\nu}:\mathbb{H}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
for mean estimation with a tuning parameter
\nu>0
:

\displaystyle\hat{L}_{\nu}(f)

\displaystyle:=\frac{\omega_{d_{r},d}}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{r_{i}}\sum_{j=1}^{r_{i}}\frac{1}{s_{ij}}\sum_{k=1}^{s_{ij}}\left(Z_{ijk}-\mathscr{P}_{\boldsymbol{R}_{ij}}f(\boldsymbol{X}_{ijk})\right)^{2}+\nu\|f\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}},

(8)

which can viewed as the negative log-posterior density under a white-noise prior on
f

[
117
]
. We remark that other type of penalties are considered in the discrete and homogeneous tomography setup – total variation
[
44
,
113
]
,
\mathcal{L}_{1}
-penalty
[
120
,
46
]
, or hybrid projection
[
14
]
.

To estimate the covariance
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
, we employ a plug-in approach: first, we estimate the second moment operator
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
, and then we define the covariance estimator as
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}:=\hat{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}-\hat{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\otimes\hat{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
. For second-order estimation, note that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[Z_{ijk}Z_{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}k^{\prime}}|(\boldsymbol{R}_{ij},\boldsymbol{X}_{ijk}),(\boldsymbol{R}_{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}},\boldsymbol{X}_{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}k^{\prime}})]

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\langle\boldsymbol{\mu},\varphi_{ijk}\rangle_{\mathbb{H}}\langle\boldsymbol{\mu},\varphi_{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}k^{\prime}}\rangle_{\mathbb{H}},&i\neq i^{\prime},\\
\langle\boldsymbol{\Gamma}\varphi_{ijk},\varphi_{ij^{\prime}k^{\prime}}\rangle_{\mathbb{H}}+\sigma_{r_{i}}^{2}\delta_{jj^{\prime}}\delta_{kk^{\prime}},&i=i^{\prime},\end{cases}

(9)

which reveals that the product of observations
Z_{ijk}Z_{ij^{\prime}k^{\prime}}
from any off-diagonal pair
(j,k)\neq(j^{\prime},k^{\prime})
provides a conditionally unbiased estimate of
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
evaluated at the corresponding orientation and location. While this is true, we restrict our analysis to cross-terms where
j\neq j^{\prime}
, which correspond to products across different projection images of the same object. This technical restriction is crucial for asymptotic analysis, because the feature maps
\varphi_{ijk}
and
\varphi_{ij^{\prime}k^{\prime}}
are
fully
independent only when
j\neq j^{\prime}
. In contrast, for
j=j^{\prime}
and
k\neq k^{\prime}
, the feature maps are merely conditionally independent given
\boldsymbol{R}_{ij}
, thus complicates the asymptotic derivation, because (
7.2
) in
Section

7.2
no longer hold unconditionally.

To simplify the notation, we merge the multi-index
(j,k)
into
J
when there is no risk of confusion. Accordingly, we denote by
\varphi_{iJJ^{\prime}}^{\otimes}\in\mathbb{H}\otimes\mathbb{H}
and
Z_{iJJ^{\prime}}\in\mathbb{R}
, instead of
\varphi_{ijk}\otimes\varphi_{ij^{\prime}k^{\prime}}
and
Z_{ijk}Z_{ij^{\prime}k^{\prime}}
, respectively. While the mean can be estimated with just one projection (
r_{i}\geq 1
) per object is enough for mean estimation, estimating the covariance requires at least two projections (
r_{i}\geq 2
) to capture the second-order interactions within the process
\mathcal{Y}_{i}
. With these considerations, the empirical risk functional for the second moment operator
\hat{L}_{\eta}^{\odot}:\mathbb{H}\otimes\mathbb{H}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
with a tuning parameter
\eta>0
is given as follow:

\displaystyle\hat{L}_{\eta}^{\odot}(G):=\frac{\omega_{d_{r},d}^{2}}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{r_{i}(r_{i}-1)}\sum_{1\leq j\neq j^{\prime}\leq r_{i}}\frac{1}{s_{ij}s_{ij^{\prime}}}\times

\displaystyle\hskip 60.00009pt\quad\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq k\leq s_{ij}\\
1\leq k^{\prime}\leq s_{ij^{\prime}}\end{subarray}}(Z_{ijk}Z_{ij^{\prime}k^{\prime}}-(\mathscr{P}_{\boldsymbol{R}_{ij}}\otimes\mathscr{P}_{\boldsymbol{R}_{ij^{\prime}}})G(\boldsymbol{X}_{ijk},\boldsymbol{X}_{ij^{\prime}k^{\prime}}))^{2}+\eta\|G\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}\otimes\mathbb{H}}.

(10)

Proposition 5.2
.

Given random orientations
\boldsymbol{R}_{ij}\in\mathbb{G}
and locations
\boldsymbol{X}_{ijk}\in\Omega^{d-d_{r}}
, define finite-dimensional subspaces of
\mathbb{H}
and
\mathbb{H}\otimes\mathbb{H}
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{H}_{\boldsymbol{F}}:=\text{span}\{\varphi_{ijk}:1\leq i\leq n,1\leq j\leq r_{i},1\leq k\leq s_{ij}\},

\displaystyle(\mathbb{H}\otimes\mathbb{H})_{\boldsymbol{F}}:=\text{span}\{\varphi_{ijk}\otimes\varphi_{ij^{\prime}k^{\prime}}:1\leq i\leq n,1\leq j\neq j^{\prime}\leq r_{i},1\leq k\leq s_{ij},1\leq k^{\prime}\leq s_{ij^{\prime}}\},

respectively. Then, for
f\in\mathbb{H}
and
G\in\mathbb{H}\otimes\mathbb{H}
,

\displaystyle f\in\mathbb{H}_{\boldsymbol{F}}^{\perp}\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\mathscr{P}_{\boldsymbol{R}_{ij}}f(\boldsymbol{X}_{ij})=0,\quad 1\leq i\leq n,1\leq j\leq r_{i},

\displaystyle G\in(\mathbb{H}\otimes\mathbb{H})_{\boldsymbol{F}}^{\perp}\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad(\mathscr{P}_{\boldsymbol{R}_{ij}}\otimes\mathscr{P}_{\boldsymbol{R}_{ij^{\prime}}})G(\boldsymbol{X}_{ij},\boldsymbol{X}_{ij^{\prime}})=0,\quad 1\leq i\leq n,1\leq j\neq j^{\prime}\leq r_{i}.

The above proposition immediately leads us to the following representer theorem.

Proposition 5.3
.

Given
\nu,\eta>0
, let the empirical risk functionals
\hat{L}_{\nu}:\mathbb{H}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
and
\hat{L}_{\eta}^{\odot}:\mathbb{H}\otimes\mathbb{H}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be defined as in (
8
) and (
5.2
).
Then, both the Tikhonov regularizers:

\hat{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{\nu}:=\argmin_{f\in\mathbb{H}}\hat{L}_{\nu}(f),\quad\hat{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}_{\eta}:=\argmin_{G\in\mathbb{H}\otimes\mathbb{H}}\hat{L}_{\eta}^{\odot}(G).

are unique, and belong to
\mathbb{H}_{\boldsymbol{F}}
and
(\mathbb{H}\otimes\mathbb{H})_{\boldsymbol{F}}
, respectively. Additionally,
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}_{\eta}
is self-adjoint.

Although the second moment operator
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}\in\mathcal{B}_{1}(\mathbb{H})
is trace-class, our estimation is carried out in the larger space of HS operators
\mathbb{H}\otimes\mathbb{H}\cong\mathcal{B}_{2}(\mathbb{H})
. Nevertheless,
Proposition

5.3
shows that the estimator
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}_{\eta}
is finite-rank and therefore belongs to
\mathcal{B}_{1}(\mathbb{H})
automatically as desired.
We proceed to find the coefficients for the estimators
\hat{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{\nu}\in\mathbb{H}_{\boldsymbol{F}}
and
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}_{\eta}\in(\mathbb{H}\otimes\mathbb{H})_{\boldsymbol{F}}
with respect to their corresponding frames. This requires defining the following observation vectors and sample-size-adjusted Gram matrices:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{w}:=\mathrm{vec}\left[\frac{Z_{ijk}}{\sqrt{r_{i}s_{ij}}}\right],\,\boldsymbol{w}^{\odot}:=\mathrm{vec}\left[\frac{Z_{ijk}Z_{ij^{\prime}k^{\prime}}}{\sqrt{r_{i}(r_{i}-1)s_{ij}s_{ij^{\prime}}}}\right]_{j\neq j^{\prime}},\,\boldsymbol{\Phi}:=\left[\frac{\langle\varphi_{i_{1}j_{1}k_{1}},\varphi_{i_{2}j_{2}k_{2}}\rangle_{\mathbb{H}}}{\sqrt{r_{i_{1}}s_{i_{1}j_{1}}}\sqrt{r_{i_{2}}s_{i_{2}j_{2}}}}\right],

(11)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Phi}^{\odot}:=\left[\frac{\langle\varphi_{i_{1}j_{1}k_{1}}\otimes\varphi_{i_{1}j^{\prime}_{1}k^{\prime}_{1}},\varphi_{i_{2}j_{2}k_{2}}\otimes\varphi_{i_{2}j^{\prime}_{2}k^{\prime}_{2}}\rangle_{\mathbb{H}\otimes\mathbb{H}}}{\sqrt{r_{i_{1}}(r_{i_{1}}-1)s_{i_{1}j_{1}}s_{i_{1}j^{\prime}_{1}}}\sqrt{r_{i_{2}}(r_{i_{2}}-1)s_{i_{2}j_{2}}s_{i_{2}j^{\prime}_{2}}}}\right]_{\begin{subarray}{c}j_{1}\neq j^{\prime}_{1}\\
j_{2}\neq j^{\prime}_{2}\end{subarray}}.

(12)

Here, the symbol
\odot
stands for a block-wise tensor product, also called the Khatri-Rao product
[
52
,
71
]
.
Note that
\boldsymbol{\Phi}
and
\boldsymbol{\Phi}^{\odot}
are both s.p.d, with their components calculated using (
S.6
).

Theorem 5.4
.

Let
\nu,\eta>0
be penalty parameters. Then, the minimizer of
\hat{L}_{\nu}:\mathbb{H}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
in (
8
) is given by

\hat{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{\nu}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{\sqrt{r_{i}}}\sum_{j=1}^{r_{i}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{s_{ij}}}\sum_{k=1}^{s_{ij}}\hat{\alpha}^{\nu}_{ijk}\varphi(\boldsymbol{R}_{ij},\boldsymbol{X}_{ijk}),\quad\hat{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{\nu}:=\mathrm{vec}[\hat{\alpha}^{\nu}_{ijk}]=\left(\boldsymbol{\Phi}+\frac{n\nu}{\omega_{d_{r},d}}\boldsymbol{I}\right)^{-1}\boldsymbol{w}.

The minimizer of
\hat{L}_{\eta}^{\odot}:\mathbb{H}\otimes\mathbb{H}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
in (
5.2
) is given by

\hat{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}_{\eta}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{\sqrt{r_{i}(r_{i}-1)}}\sum_{1\leq j\neq j^{\prime}\leq r_{i}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{s_{ij}s_{ij^{\prime}}}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq k\leq s_{ij}\\
1\leq k^{\prime}\leq s_{ij^{\prime}}\end{subarray}}\hat{\alpha}^{\odot}_{ijkj^{\prime}k^{\prime}}\varphi(\boldsymbol{R}_{ij},\boldsymbol{X}_{ijk})\otimes\varphi(\boldsymbol{R}_{ij^{\prime}},\boldsymbol{X}_{ij^{\prime}k^{\prime}}),

where
\hat{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{\eta}^{\odot}:=\mathrm{vec}[\hat{\alpha}^{\odot}_{ijkj^{\prime}k^{\prime}}]=(\boldsymbol{\Phi}^{\odot}+n\eta/\omega_{d_{r},d}^{2}\boldsymbol{I})^{-1}\boldsymbol{w}^{\odot}
.

By
Proposition

A.6
, the evaluations of
\hat{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{\nu}:\Omega^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
and
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}_{\eta}:\Omega^{d}\times\Omega^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
are given by

\displaystyle\hat{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{\nu}(\boldsymbol{z})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{\sqrt{r_{i}}}\sum_{j=1}^{r_{i}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{s_{ij}}}\sum_{k=1}^{s_{ij}}\hat{\alpha}^{\nu}_{ijk}\mathscr{P}_{\boldsymbol{R}_{ij}}k_{\boldsymbol{z}}(\boldsymbol{X}_{ijk}),

\displaystyle\hat{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}_{\eta}(\boldsymbol{z},\boldsymbol{z}^{\prime})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{1\leq j\neq j^{\prime}\leq r_{i}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq k\leq s_{ij}\\
1\leq k^{\prime}\leq s_{ij^{\prime}}\end{subarray}}\frac{\hat{\alpha}^{\odot}_{ijkj^{\prime}k^{\prime}}}{\sqrt{r_{i}(r_{i}-1)s_{ij}s_{ij^{\prime}}}}\mathscr{P}_{\boldsymbol{R}_{ij}}k_{\boldsymbol{z}}(\boldsymbol{X}_{ijk})\mathscr{P}_{\boldsymbol{R}_{ij^{\prime}}}k_{\boldsymbol{z}^{\prime}}(\boldsymbol{X}_{ij^{\prime}k^{\prime}}),

and thus,
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}_{\eta}:\Omega^{d}\times\Omega^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is a symmetric bivariate function:
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}_{\eta}(\boldsymbol{z},\boldsymbol{z}^{\prime})=\hat{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}_{\eta}(\boldsymbol{z}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{z})
.

Once we obtain
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}_{\eta}
and
\hat{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{\nu}
for some
\nu,\eta>0
, we estimate the covariance using the plug-in formula:
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{\nu,\eta}=\hat{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}_{\eta}-\hat{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{\nu}\otimes\hat{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{\nu}
, or equivalently,

\hat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{\nu,\eta}(\boldsymbol{z},\boldsymbol{z}^{\prime})=\hat{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}_{\eta}(\boldsymbol{z},\boldsymbol{z}^{\prime})-\hat{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{\nu}(\boldsymbol{z})\hat{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{\nu}(\boldsymbol{z}^{\prime})
, which remains symmetric.
However, a key issue arises with this approach. As discussed in
Remark

B.1
, the second moment estimator
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}_{\eta}
is not s.p.d. unless it is identically zero
[
118
]
. Consequently, the covariance estimator
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{\nu,\eta}
also fails to be s.p.d. in general. This problem stems from the kernel method with the exclusive use of off-diagonal product pairs in the estimation procedure, and
not
from the inverse problem setup.
In practice, however, this deviation from the s.p.d. property is negligible as our estimators are consistent. Still, in finite-sample scenarios, one may project
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{\nu,\eta}
onto the closed convex cone of s.p.d. tensors, which preserves the statistical consistency and does not alter the convergence rate in
Section

7.2
.

5.3
Numerical Challenges

While
Theorem

5.4
provides elegant least-squares solutions, a naïve implementation is computationally infeasible. Two main bottlenecks arise: solving the normal equation for the covariance and performing the subsequent kernel PCA. This section briefly discusses these challenges and our numerical strategies for overcoming them, with further details available in Supplement
B
and recent work
[
118
]
.

Recall from
Theorem

5.4
that the second-moment estimation requires solving the linear system:
(\boldsymbol{\Phi}^{\odot}+n\eta/\omega_{d_{r},d}^{2}\boldsymbol{I})\hat{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{\eta}^{\odot}=\boldsymbol{w}^{\odot}
.
The problem with a direct, factorization-based approach is that it requires explicitly forming the covariance Gram matrix
\boldsymbol{\Phi}^{\odot}
, which is often computationally prohibitive. For example, in our simulation with
n=100
particles,
r=5
tilts, and
s=10
locations per tilt, this Gram matrix has over
50
billion entries. Storing it in double-precision format would require
377
GB of memory, compared to just
191
MB for the mean Gram matrix
\boldsymbol{\Phi}
.
The solution is to use a
matrix-free
iterative solver – we never explicitly form the Khatri-Rao product
\boldsymbol{\Phi}^{\odot}
, as its action can be implemented only using much smaller matrix
\boldsymbol{\Phi}
. The
TReK
algorithm
[
118
]
, a tensorized version of Krylov subspace method
[
87
,
32
,
5
]
, is designed to solve such least-squares problems.

A second challenge arises when performing kernel PCA to analyze the estimated covariance
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{\nu,\eta}=\hat{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}_{\eta}-\hat{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{\nu}\otimes\hat{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{\nu}
. After arranging the solution vector
\hat{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{\eta}^{\odot}
into the block-diagonal matrix
\hat{\boldsymbol{A}}_{\eta}
,
the kernel PCA reduces to the generalized eigenvalue problem, see
Theorem

B.2
:

[\hat{\boldsymbol{A}}_{\eta}-\hat{\boldsymbol{a}}_{\nu}\hat{\boldsymbol{a}}_{\nu}^{\top}]\boldsymbol{\Phi}\boldsymbol{V}=\boldsymbol{V}\boldsymbol{\Lambda},\quad\boldsymbol{V}^{*}\boldsymbol{\Phi}\boldsymbol{V}=\boldsymbol{I},

an equation arising frequently in statistics. A common two-step approach to this problem is to first computing the usual eigendecomposition
\boldsymbol{\Phi}^{1/2}[\hat{\boldsymbol{A}}_{\eta}-\hat{\boldsymbol{a}}_{\nu}\hat{\boldsymbol{a}}_{\nu}^{\top}]\boldsymbol{\Phi}^{1/2}=\boldsymbol{U}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\boldsymbol{U}^{\top}
, and then recover the generalized eigenvectors
\boldsymbol{V}
via
\boldsymbol{U}=\boldsymbol{\Phi}^{1/2}\boldsymbol{V}

[
9
,
85
]
. However, this requires computing the matrix square root
\boldsymbol{\Phi}^{1/2}
, which is slow and can be numerically unstable if
\boldsymbol{\Phi}
is ill-conditioned or has a low numerical rank
[
72
]
.
To avoid this, we again use a matrix-free iterative method based on the Lanczos tridiagonalization
[
56
,
74
]
, which is faster and more stable than the two-step approach, especially when we only need to extract the leading eigenvalues and eigenvectors
(\lambda_{k},\boldsymbol{v}_{k})
, often called the Ritz pairs
[
45
,
14
]
.

6
Mercer’s Theory in Inverse Problems

This section describes the interplay between the Mercer decomposition and the inverse problem, which is essential for developing asymptotic theory. In
Section

A.1
, we explain how the theory reduces to the classical FDA setup in the absence of inverse problems.

Given a sinogram kernel
K
on
\Omega^{d}
, we first define the induced reproducing kernel for the space of projection images:

\displaystyle\tilde{K}((R,\boldsymbol{x}),(R^{\prime},\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime})):=\langle\varphi(R,\boldsymbol{x}),\varphi(R^{\prime},\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime})\rangle_{\mathbb{H}},\quad R,R^{\prime}\in\mathbb{G},\,\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}\in\Omega^{d-d_{r}},

(13)

Although not required for the asymptotic theory, we shortly remark another powerful advantage of the RKHS framework. In
Theorem

A.8
, we show that
\tilde{K}
is also a
\mathbb{G}
-invariant Mercer kernel. More importantly, when considered as an operator between RKHSs,
\mathscr{P}:\mathbb{H}(K)\rightarrow\mathbb{H}(\tilde{K})
is a well-defined, contractive, and surjective map whose adjoint is an isometry.

Consider the integral operator
\mathscr{T}_{K}\in\mathcal{B}_{1}(\mathcal{L}_{2}(\Omega^{d},W_{0,d}))
associated with
K
:

\mathscr{T}_{K}f(\cdot):=\int_{\Omega^{d}}K(\boldsymbol{z},\cdot)f(\boldsymbol{z})W_{0,d}(\boldsymbol{z})\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{z}.

The Mercer theorem reveals that
\mathscr{T}_{K}
is s.p.d. and trace-class, and
\mathbb{H}=\mathbb{H}(K)
is the range space of the HS operator

(\mathscr{T}_{K})^{1/2}\in\mathcal{B}_{2}(\mathcal{L}_{2}(\Omega^{d},W_{0,d}))
, equipped with the range norm
[
89
]
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{H}=\{(\mathscr{T}_{K})^{1/2}h:h\in\mathcal{L}_{2}(\Omega^{d},W_{0,d})\},\quad\|f\|_{\mathbb{H}}=\inf\{\|h\|_{\mathcal{L}_{2}(\Omega^{d},W_{0,d})}:f=(\mathscr{T}_{K})^{1/2}h\},

It follows that the continuous embedding
\imath:\mathbb{H}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{L}_{2}(\Omega^{d},W_{0,d})
is a HS operator satisfying

\mathscr{T}_{K}=\imath\circ\imath^{*},\quad\VERT\imath\VERT_{2}^{2}=\VERT\imath^{*}\circ\imath\VERT_{1}=\VERT\mathscr{T}_{K}\VERT_{1}=\int_{\Omega^{d}}K(\boldsymbol{z},\boldsymbol{z})W_{0,d}(\boldsymbol{z})\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{z}<+\infty.

This implies that when we restrict the sinogram operator’s domain to
\mathbb{H}(K)
, the embedded operator is also a HS operator:

\mathscr{P}\circ\imath:\mathbb{H}(K)\hookrightarrow\mathcal{L}_{2}(\Omega^{d},W_{0,d})\rightarrow\mathcal{L}_{2}(\mathbb{G}\times\Omega^{d-d_{r}},W_{d_{r},d}),f\mapsto\mathscr{P}f.

Proposition 6.1
.

Let
\mathscr{P}
be a sinogram operator, and
K:\Omega^{d}\times\Omega^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be a sinogram kernel, and
\tilde{K}:(\mathbb{G}\times\Omega^{d-d_{r}})\times(\mathbb{G}\times\Omega^{d-d_{r}})\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be an induced kernel as in (
13
). Then,

\mathscr{T}_{\tilde{K}}=(\mathscr{P}\circ\imath)(\mathscr{P}\circ\imath)^{*}=\mathscr{P}\mathscr{T}_{K}\mathscr{P}^{*}\in\mathcal{B}_{1}(\mathcal{L}_{2}(\mathbb{G}\times\Omega^{d-d_{r}},W_{d_{r},d})).

Consequently, consider the singular value decomposition (SVD) of
\mathscr{P}\circ\imath
:

\mathscr{P}\circ\imath=\sum_{l=1}^{\infty}\tau_{l}\sum_{m=1}^{N(l)}g_{lm}\otimes e_{lm}:\mathbb{H}(K)\rightarrow\mathcal{L}_{2}(\mathbb{G}\times\Omega^{d-d_{r}},W_{d_{r},d}),

where
\tau_{1}\geq\tau_{2}\geq\dots\geq 0
are the non-zero singular values,
N(l)
is the multiplicity of
\tau_{l}
, and
e_{lm}\in\mathbb{H}(K)
,
g_{lm}\in\mathcal{L}_{2}(\mathbb{G}\times\Omega^{d-d_{r}},W_{d_{r},d})
have unit norms. Observe that
\{g_{lm}:l\in\mathbb{N},1\leq m\leq N(l)\}
and
\{e_{lm}:l\in\mathbb{N},1\leq m\leq N(l)\}
form CONSs for
\overline{\mathcal{R}(\mathscr{T}_{\tilde{K}})}
and
\overline{\mathcal{R}(\mathscr{K})}
, respectively. However,
e_{lm}
’s may not be orthogonal in
\mathcal{L}_{2}(\Omega^{d},W_{0,d})
, unless
\mathscr{P}^{*}\mathscr{P}
and
\mathscr{T}_{K}
commute. Additionally, note that
g_{lm}=\tau_{l}^{-1}\mathscr{P}e_{lm}
, hence

\sup_{R,\boldsymbol{x}}|g_{lm}(R,\boldsymbol{x})|=\tau_{l}^{-1}\sup_{R,\boldsymbol{x}}|\mathscr{P}e_{lm}(R,\boldsymbol{x})|\leq\tau_{l}^{-1}\|e_{lm}\|_{\mathbb{H}}\sup_{R,\boldsymbol{x}}\|\varphi(R,\boldsymbol{x})\|_{\mathbb{H}}=\tau_{l}^{-1}B.

(14)

Also, observe from the intertwining property in
Proposition

A.4
that

g_{lm}(R,\boldsymbol{x})=\rho(R)g_{lm}(I,\boldsymbol{x})=\tau_{l}^{-1}\mathscr{P}[\rho(R)e_{lm}](I,\boldsymbol{x})=\tau_{l}^{-1}\mathscr{P}_{I}[\rho(R)e_{lm}](\boldsymbol{x}).

It follows from
Proposition

6.1
that

\sum_{l}\tau_{l}^{2}N(l)=\VERT\mathscr{T}_{\tilde{K}}\VERT_{1}=\int_{\Omega^{d-d_{r}}}\tilde{K}((R,\boldsymbol{x}),(R,\boldsymbol{x}))W_{d_{r},d}(\boldsymbol{x})\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}<+\infty,

which is independent of the choice of
R\in\mathbb{G}
due to
\mathbb{G}
-invariance of
\tilde{K}
. Consequently,
\mathscr{K}:=(\mathscr{P}\circ\imath)^{*}(\mathscr{P}\circ\imath)\in\mathcal{B}_{1}(\mathbb{H})
, a central operator in asymptotic theory, admits the spectral decomposition:

\mathscr{K}=\sum_{l=1}^{\infty}\tau_{l}^{2}\sum_{m=1}^{N(l)}e_{lm}\otimes_{\mathbb{H}}e_{lm}.

This operator gives the
\mathcal{L}_{2}
-inner product between sinograms in the following sense:

\langle f_{1},\mathscr{K}f_{2}\rangle_{\mathbb{H}}=\langle\mathscr{P}f_{1},\mathscr{P}f_{2}\rangle_{\mathcal{L}_{2}(\mathbb{G}\times\Omega^{d-d_{r}},W_{d_{r},d})},\quad f_{1},f_{2}\in\mathbb{H}.

(15)

Additionally,
\mathscr{K}/\omega_{d_{r},d}
is precisely the expectation of the tensorized random feature map:

Proposition 6.2
.

Let
\mathscr{P}
be a sinogram operator and
K
be a sinogram kernel. Let
\boldsymbol{R}\sim\mathbb{P}_{\boldsymbol{R}}
and
\boldsymbol{X}\sim\mathbb{P}_{\boldsymbol{X}}
be independent. Then, the operator
\mathscr{K}
satisfies

\displaystyle\mathscr{K}=\omega_{d_{r},d}\mathbb{E}[\varphi(I,\boldsymbol{X})\otimes\varphi(I,\boldsymbol{X})]=\omega_{d_{r},d}\mathbb{E}[\varphi(\boldsymbol{R},\boldsymbol{X})\otimes\varphi(\boldsymbol{R},\boldsymbol{X})].

Moreover,
\mathscr{K}\in\mathcal{B}_{1}(\mathbb{H})
is s.p.d. with the norm
\VERT\mathscr{K}\VERT_{1}=\omega_{d_{r},d}\mathbb{E}[\|\varphi(\boldsymbol{R},\boldsymbol{X})\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}]
.

We now explain how
\mathbb{G}
-invariance yields the orthogonal decompositions associated with the SVD of
\mathscr{P}\circ\imath
. For each
l\in\mathbb{N}
, define the finite-dimensional subspaces

\displaystyle\mathbb{H}_{l}:

\displaystyle=\{f\in\mathbb{H}:\mathscr{K}f=\tau_{l}^{2}f\}=\spann\{e_{lm}:m=1,2,\dots,N(l)\},

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{H}}_{l}:

\displaystyle=\{g\in\mathcal{L}_{2}(\mathbb{G}\times\Omega^{d-d_{r}},W_{d_{r},d}):\mathscr{T}_{\tilde{K}}g=\tau_{l}^{2}g\}=\spann\{g_{lm}:m=1,2,\dots,N(l)\}.

Since
\mathbb{H}_{l}
’s are
\mathbb{G}
-invariant subspaces with respect to the unitary representation
(\rho,\mathbb{H})
from
Proposition

A.4
,
[\rho(R)]_{\{e_{lm}:1\leq m\leq N(l)\}}\in SO(N(l))
for any
R\in\mathbb{G}
, similar to the Wigner D-matrix
[
115
,
17
]
. We can draw the same conclusion for
\tilde{\mathcal{H}}_{l}:=\mathscr{P}(\mathbb{H}_{l})
, hence the following orthogonal decomposition of the output space:

\mathcal{L}_{2}(\mathbb{G}\times\Omega^{d-d_{r}},W_{d_{r},d})=\bigoplus_{l=1}^{\infty}\tilde{\mathcal{H}}_{l}\oplus\mathcal{R}(\mathscr{P}\circ\imath)^{\perp}.

If we view the finite-dimensional
\mathbb{G}
-invariant subspace
(\tilde{\mathcal{H}}_{l},W_{d_{r},d})
as an RKHS, equipped with the weighted
\mathcal{L}_{2}
norm, the corresponding reproducing kernel
\tilde{K}_{l}:(\mathbb{G}\times\Omega^{d-d_{r}})\times(\mathbb{G}\times\Omega^{d-d_{r}})\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is given by

\tilde{K}_{l}((R_{1},\boldsymbol{x}_{1}),(R_{2},\boldsymbol{x}_{2})):=\sum_{m=1}^{N(l)}g_{lm}(R_{1},\boldsymbol{x}_{1})g_{lm}(R_{2},\boldsymbol{x}_{2}),

(16)

which is well-defined by virtue of (
14
). Then, the Mercer decomposition of the induced reproducing kernel can be expressed as
\tilde{K}=\sum_{l=1}^{\infty}\tau_{l}^{2}\tilde{K}_{l}
.

Proposition 6.3
.

For each
l\in\mathbb{N}
,
\tilde{K}_{l}
satisfies the reproducing property:

\displaystyle\tilde{K}_{l}((R_{1},\boldsymbol{x}_{1}),(R_{2},\boldsymbol{x}_{2}))=\langle\tilde{K}_{l}((R_{1},\boldsymbol{x}_{1}),\cdot),\tilde{K}_{l}((R_{2},\boldsymbol{x}_{2}),\cdot)\rangle_{\mathcal{L}_{2}(\mathbb{G}\times\Omega^{d-d_{r}},W_{d_{r},d})}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{G}}\int_{\Omega^{d-d_{r}}}\tilde{K}_{l}((R_{1},\boldsymbol{x}_{1}),(R_{3},\boldsymbol{x}_{3}))\tilde{K}_{l}((R_{2},\boldsymbol{x}_{2}),(R_{3},\boldsymbol{x}_{3}))W_{d_{r},d}(\boldsymbol{x}_{3})\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}_{3}\mathrm{d}R_{3}.

Moreover, the associated integral operator
\mathscr{T}_{\tilde{K}_{l}}\in\mathcal{B}_{\infty}(\mathcal{L}_{2}(\mathbb{G}\times\Omega^{d-d_{r}},W_{d_{r},d}))
is the orthogonal projection onto
\tilde{\mathcal{H}}_{l}
. The kernel
\tilde{K}_{l}
is
\mathbb{G}
-invariant, and satisfies

\int_{\Omega^{d-d_{r}}}\tilde{K}_{l}((R,\boldsymbol{x}),(R,\boldsymbol{x}))W_{d_{r},d}(\boldsymbol{x})\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}=N(l).

7
Asymptotic Analysis

To provide the uniform rate of convergence of our estimators, we require an additional assumption:

(A)

There exists some
\tilde{B}>0
such that, the
l
-th kernel
\tilde{K}_{l}
in (
16
) induced by
K
satisfies

\frac{\tilde{K}_{l}((I,\boldsymbol{x}),(I,\boldsymbol{x}))}{N(l)}\leq\tilde{B},\quad l\in\mathbb{N},\ \boldsymbol{x}\in\Omega^{d-d_{r}}.

Since the kernel
\tilde{K}_{l}
is
\mathbb{G}
-invariant and s.p.d., condition (A) is equivalent to

\frac{\tilde{K}_{l}((R,\boldsymbol{x}),(R^{\prime},\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}))}{N(l)}\leq\tilde{B},\quad l\in\mathbb{N},\ R,R^{\prime}\in\mathbb{G},\ \boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}\in\Omega^{d-d_{r}}.

In view of
Proposition

6.3
, condition (A) amounts to requiring that the supremum norm of
\tilde{K}_{l}
is uniformly bounded across
l\in\mathbb{N}
in terms of its
\mathcal{L}_{1}
-norm along diagonal pairs. Heuristically, as
l\in\mathbb{N}
grows, each singular function
g_{lm}
exhibits higher frequency oscillations; however, the growth in the dimension
N(l)
counterbalances this fluctuation in the construction of
\tilde{K}_{l}
. For instance, in
Remark

A.11
, although each spherical harmonic of degree
l
becomes more oscillatory with increasing degree
l
, the associated reproducing kernel still satisfies (A). More generally, if the group action of
\mathbb{G}
is transitive, then
\tilde{K}_{l}((I,\boldsymbol{x}),(I,\boldsymbol{x}))
is constant along diagonal pairs. In this case, (A) is automatically satisfied for any sinogram kernel
K
:

\tilde{K}_{l}((I,\boldsymbol{x}),(I,\boldsymbol{x}))=\omega_{d_{r},d}^{-1}N(l).

On the other hand, when the unitary group action on the domain is not transitive (like the action of
SO(d)
on
\mathbb{B}^{d}
for the X-ray transform), one may take a different approach. If we have explicit knowledge of the SVD of
\mathscr{P}:\mathcal{L}_{2}(\Omega^{d},W_{0,d})\to\mathcal{L}_{2}(\mathbb{G}\times\Omega^{d-d_{r}},W_{d_{r},d})
:

\mathscr{P}=\sum_{l=1}^{\infty}\lambda_{l}\sum_{m=1}^{N(l)}g_{lm}\otimes f_{lm}.

we can construct a compatible Mercer kernel using the singular functions
f_{lm}
:

K(\boldsymbol{z}_{1},\boldsymbol{z}_{2})=\sum_{l=1}^{\infty}\zeta_{l}^{2}\sum_{m=1}^{N(l)}f_{lm}(\boldsymbol{z}_{1})f_{lm}(\boldsymbol{z}_{2}),

where
\sum_{l=1}^{\infty}\zeta_{l}^{2}N(l)<+\infty
, so that
\mathscr{P}
and
\mathscr{T}_{K}
are simultaneously singular value decomposable, i.e.,
\mathscr{P}^{*}\mathscr{P}
and
\mathscr{T}_{K}
commute. In this setup, the coefficients satisfy
\tau_{l}=\lambda_{l}\zeta_{l}
and the validity of the assumption (A) for the
l
-th kernel
\tilde{K}_{l}
in (
16
) depends solely on the SVD of
\mathscr{P}
, as illustrated in
Example

C.1
for the Radon transform.

7.1
Asymptotic Behavior of the Mean Estimator

We present the uniform convergence rates of our mean estimator both in input and output levels, which are
\|\hat{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{\nu_{n}}-\boldsymbol{\mu}\|_{\mathbb{H}}
and
\|\mathscr{P}\hat{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{\nu_{n}}-\mathscr{P}\boldsymbol{\mu}\|_{\mathcal{L}_{2}(\mathbb{G}\times\Omega^{d-d_{r}},W_{d_{r},d})}
, respectively. Let us define the empirical operator

\displaystyle\hat{\mathscr{K}}:=\frac{\omega_{d_{r},d}}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{r_{i}}\sum_{j=1}^{r_{i}}\frac{1}{s_{ij}}\sum_{k=1}^{s_{ij}}\varphi_{ijk}\otimes\varphi_{ijk}\in\mathcal{B}_{\infty}(\mathbb{H}).

Then, it is straightforward from
Proposition

6.2
that
\mathscr{K}=\mathbb{E}[\hat{\mathscr{K}}]
. The decomposition

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[Z_{ijk}-\langle\varphi_{ijk},f\rangle_{\mathbb{H}}]^{2}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[(Z_{ijk}-\langle\varphi_{ijk},\boldsymbol{\mu}\rangle_{\mathbb{H}})+\langle\varphi_{ijk},\boldsymbol{\mu}-f\rangle_{\mathbb{H}}]^{2}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[(Z_{ijk}-\langle\varphi_{ijk},\boldsymbol{\mu}\rangle_{\mathbb{H}})^{2}]+\mathbb{E}[\langle\varphi_{ijk},\boldsymbol{\mu}-f\rangle_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}],

yields the expectation of empirical risk functional
\hat{L}_{\nu}
in (
8
) as

\displaystyle L_{\nu}(f):

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[\hat{L}_{\nu}(f)]=\omega_{d_{r},d}\mathbb{E}[\left(W_{111}-\langle\varphi_{111},\boldsymbol{\mu}\rangle_{\mathbb{H}}\right)^{2}]+\langle\mathscr{K}(\boldsymbol{\mu}-f),\boldsymbol{\mu}-f\rangle_{\mathbb{H}}+\nu\|f\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}

\displaystyle=\omega_{d_{r},d}\mathbb{E}[\left(W_{111}-\mathscr{P}_{\boldsymbol{R}_{11}}\boldsymbol{\mu}(\boldsymbol{X}_{111})\right)^{2}]+\langle\mathscr{K}(\boldsymbol{\mu}-f),\boldsymbol{\mu}-f\rangle_{\mathbb{H}}+\nu\|f\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}},

where the first term does not depend on
f\in\mathbb{H}
. By strong convexity, its minimizer is unique:

\bar{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{\nu}:=\argmin_{f\in\mathbb{H}}L_{\nu}(f).

Subsequently, we get explicit expressions for
\hat{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{\nu}
and
\bar{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{\nu}
similar to ridge regression
[
42
]
:

\hat{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{\nu}=(\hat{\mathscr{K}}+\nu I)^{-1}\mathcal{Z}_{n},\quad\bar{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{\nu}=(\mathscr{K}+\nu I)^{-1}\mathscr{K}\boldsymbol{\mu},

where

\mathcal{Z}_{n}:=\frac{\omega_{d_{r},d}}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{r_{i}}\sum_{j=1}^{r_{i}}\frac{1}{s_{ij}}\sum_{k=1}^{s_{ij}}Z_{ijk}\varphi_{ijk}\in\mathbb{H}.

Thus, it follows that

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}-\bar{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{\nu}=(\mathscr{K}+\nu I)^{-1}(\mathscr{K}+\nu I)\boldsymbol{\mu}-\nu(\mathscr{K}+\nu I)^{-1}\boldsymbol{\mu}=\nu(\mathscr{K}+\nu I)^{-1}\boldsymbol{\mu}.

(17)

Before presenting the results, we argue why a source condition on
\boldsymbol{\mu}
is necessary if we aim to obtain an asymptotic result at the input level
\|\hat{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{\nu_{n}}-\boldsymbol{\mu}\|_{\mathbb{H}}
. Observe from
Proposition

5.2
that
\varphi(R,\boldsymbol{x})\in\mathcal{N}(\mathscr{K})^{\perp}=\overline{\mathcal{R}(\mathscr{K})}
for any
R\in\mathbb{G}
and
\boldsymbol{x}\in\Omega^{d-d_{r}}
, and therefore their finite linear combination
\hat{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{\nu_{n}}
must also lie in
\overline{\mathcal{R}(\mathscr{K})}
. Thus, a
minimal
requirement for the consistency of
\hat{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{\nu_{n}}
is that
\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\overline{\mathcal{R}(\mathscr{K})}
; otherwise, the model would necessarily be misspecified.

Although it is true that
\|\bar{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{\nu_{n}}-\boldsymbol{\mu}\|_{\mathbb{H}}\to 0
as the
\nu_{n}\rightarrow 0
for a
single
fixed
\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\overline{\mathcal{R}(\mathscr{K})}
(see Theorem 6.2.3 in
[
42
]
), a closer examination using (
17
) shows that

\sup\{\|\bar{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{\nu}-\boldsymbol{\mu}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}:\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\overline{\mathcal{R}(\mathscr{K})},\|\boldsymbol{\mu}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\leq M_{1}\}=M_{1},\quad M_{1}>0,\nu>0,

which implies that mere boundedness of
\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\mathbb{H}
is insufficient to guarantee
uniform
consistency of
\bar{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{\nu_{n}}
. This motivates the introduction of a source condition, parameterized by
\kappa_{1},M_{1}
to control the regularity of
\boldsymbol{\mu}
, and by
M_{2}
to control the heterogeneity of
\mathcal{Y}_{i}
:

Definition 7.1

(Source regularity)
.

For
0\leq\kappa_{1}\leq 1/2
and
M_{1},M_{2}>0
, we say that a second-order
\mathbb{H}
-valued process
\mathcal{Y}
belongs to the class
\mathcal{S}_{1}(\kappa_{1},M_{1},M_{2})
if

\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\{\mathscr{K}^{\kappa_{1}}\delta:\delta\in\mathbb{H},\|\delta\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\leq M_{1}\}\subsetneq\mathbb{H},\quad\VERT\boldsymbol{\Gamma}\VERT_{1}=\mathbb{E}[\|\mathcal{Y}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}]\leq M_{2}.

When
\kappa_{1}=0
, we simply write
\mathcal{S}_{1}(\kappa_{1},M_{1},M_{2})
in place of
\mathcal{S}_{1}(M_{1},M_{2})
.

Since
\mathscr{K}\in\mathcal{B}_{1}(\mathbb{H})
is a trace-class operator, increasing the value of
\kappa_{1}\geq 0
imposes greater smoothness on the mean function
\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\mathbb{H}
. However, once
\kappa_{1}\geq 1/2
, further increasing
\kappa_{1}
does not improve the convergence rate; see the discussion preceding
Section

C.4
for details. Moreover, the Douglas factorization lemma
[
19
]
implies that

\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\{\mathscr{K}^{\kappa_{1}}\delta:\delta\in\mathbb{H},\|\delta\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\leq M_{1}\}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad M_{1}\mathscr{K}^{2\kappa_{1}}\succeq\boldsymbol{\mu}\otimes\boldsymbol{\mu},

(18)

where
\succeq
denotes the Loewner order for operators. In the maximally smooth case where
\kappa_{1}=1/2
, relation (
18
) can be further expressed, using (
15
), as

\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\{\mathscr{K}^{1/2}\delta:\delta\in\mathbb{H},\|\delta\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\leq M_{1}\}\,\Leftrightarrow\,\langle\boldsymbol{\mu},f\rangle_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}\leq M_{1}\|\mathscr{P}f\|_{\mathcal{L}_{2}(\mathbb{G}\times\Omega^{d-d_{r}},W_{d_{r},d})}^{2},\,f\in\mathbb{H}(K).

The heteroscedasticity of the errors introduces a dependence of the convergence rate on two key quantities:

\overline{r}:=\left[\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{r_{i}}\right]^{-1},\quad\overline{r}^{\sigma^{2}}:=\left[\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{r_{i}}\left(\frac{\sigma_{r_{i}}^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}\right)\right]^{-1}.

We refer to these quantities as the
effective tilt number
and the
noise-weighted effective tilt number
, respectively. They represent the harmonic mean of the number of tilts, with the second term adjusted for the noise variance in each measurement. In the second term, we normalize by a baseline variance
\sigma^{2}
, which is set to
\sigma_{1}^{2}
for reference.

To make the asymptotic results more interpretable, we assume that the eigenvalues
\tau_{l}^{2}
of
\mathscr{K}
(or
\mathscr{T}_{\tilde{K}}
) decay in a polynomial speed, while their multiplicities
N(l)
grow polynomially:

\tau_{l}\asymp l^{-p},\quad N(l)\asymp l^{q},\quad p,q\geq 0.

Recall that
\VERT\mathscr{K}\VERT_{1}=\sum_{l}\tau_{l}^{2}N(l)<\infty
must hold, which is equivalent to the condition
2p>q+1
. This incorporates the familiar Sobolev-type condition
p>1/2
, as discussed in
Example

A.9
. The parameter
p
quantifies the degree of smoothness, while
q
typically scales with the intrinsic dimension
d
of the domain, manifesting the curse of dimensionality in our asymptotic bounds. For general asymptotic results stated in terms of Schatten norms, without assuming specific decay or growth rates for
\tau_{l}
and
N(l)
, see
Theorem

C.5
. In the case where
\mathscr{P}_{I}=I
, the following theorem subsumes the known results for kernel regression
[
6
,
9
,
8
]
, matching the optimal convergence rates established in
[
7
]
.

Theorem 7.2
.

Let
M_{1},M_{2}>0
. Assume (A) and that the eigenvalues
\tau_{l}^{2}
of
\mathscr{T}_{\tilde{K}}
satisfies
\tau_{l}\asymp l^{-p}
with multiplicities
N(l)\asymp l^{q}
for some
p,q\geq 0
with
2p>q+1
. If the decaying speed of the penalty parameter is
\nu_{n}\asymp[n\cdot\min(\overline{r},\overline{r}^{\sigma^{2}})]^{-2p/(2p+q+1)}
, then it holds that

\displaystyle\lim_{D\rightarrow\infty}\limsup_{n\rightarrow\infty}\sup_{\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{Y}}\in\mathcal{S}_{1}(M_{1},M_{2})}\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{Y}}

\displaystyle\left[\|\mathscr{P}\hat{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{\nu_{n}}-\mathscr{P}\boldsymbol{\mu}\|_{\mathcal{L}_{2}(\mathbb{G}\times\Omega^{d-d_{r}},W_{d_{r},d})}^{2}>\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\left.D((n\cdot\min(\overline{r},\overline{r}^{\sigma^{2}}))^{-\frac{2p}{2p+q+1}}+n^{-1})\right]=0.

We now unpack the implications of
Theorem

7.2
. First, the convergence rate for the estimated sinogram depends on the effective tilt numbers,
not
on the locational sampling frequency.
Intuitively, if only a single viewing angle is available, one cannot recover the object’s structure nor its full sinogram across different orientations, no matter how densely that single view is sampled. Nevertheless, a careful look at the proof shows that higher sampling leads to sharper non-asymptotic bounds.

Additionally,
Theorem

7.2
reveals a
phase transition
based on how the number of tilts compares to the number of functions
n

[
6
,
9
,
7
]
. As
1/2<2p/(2p+q+1)<1
, the convergence rate is always slower than
(n\cdot\min(\overline{r},\overline{r}^{\sigma^{2}}))^{-1}+n^{-1}
, yet faster than
(n\cdot\min(\overline{r},\overline{r}^{\sigma^{2}}))^{-1/2}+n^{-1}
. Then, we see two regimes depending on the tilt numbers:

•

Sparse Tilts: In the regime where
\min(\overline{r},\overline{r}^{\sigma^{2}})=O(n^{\frac{q+1}{2p}})
, the convergence rate is

\|\mathscr{P}\hat{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{\nu_{n}}-\mathscr{P}\boldsymbol{\mu}\|_{\mathcal{L}_{2}(\mathbb{G}\times\Omega^{d-d_{r}},W_{d_{r},d})}^{2}=O_{\mathbb{P}}\left((\min(\overline{r},\overline{r}^{\sigma^{2}}))^{-2p/(2p+q+1)}\right).

•

Dense Tilts: In the regime where
\min(\overline{r},\overline{r}^{\sigma^{2}})\gg n^{\frac{q+1}{2p}}
, we have enough tilt information and achieve a parametric rate that no longer depends on the effective tilt numbers:

\|\mathscr{P}\hat{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{\nu_{n}}-\mathscr{P}\boldsymbol{\mu}\|_{\mathcal{L}_{2}(\mathbb{G}\times\Omega^{d-d_{r}},W_{d_{r},d})}^{2}=O_{\mathbb{P}}\left(n^{-1}\right).

Finally, the theorem demonstrates another phase transition, this one related to the heteroscedastic noise. When the noise function
r\mapsto\sigma_{r}^{2}
is increasing, the convergence rate depends on the
noise-weighted
effective tilt number (
\overline{r}^{\sigma^{2}}
), making the rate slower than in the homoscedastic case. This is reasonable, as collecting more tilts introduces more noise into the overall dataset. In the extreme case where the noise variance grows faster than linearly with the number of tilts, the convergence rate can actually degrade as more tilts are added. This result provides a theoretical basis for the practical trade-off between acquiring more viewing angles and the corresponding degradation in image quality. As we will show later, the covariance estimation is even more susceptible to this noise growth.

Note that the preceding analysis of the output sinogram error does not require the source regularity parameter
\kappa_{1}
. However, to analyze the convergence rate of the object itself in the input space, this parameter becomes necessary.

Theorem 7.3
.

Let
0\leq\kappa_{1}\leq 1/2
and
M_{1},M_{2}>0
. Assume (A) and that the eigenvalues
\tau_{l}^{2}
of
\mathscr{T}_{\tilde{K}}
satisfies
\tau_{l}\asymp l^{-p}
with multiplicities
N(l)\asymp l^{q}
for some
p,q\geq 0
with
2p>q+1
. If the decaying speed of the penalty parameter is
\nu_{n}\asymp(n\cdot\min(\overline{r},\overline{r}^{\sigma^{2}}))^{-(\frac{2p+q+1}{2p}+2\kappa_{1})^{-1}}
, then

\displaystyle\lim_{D\rightarrow\infty}\limsup_{n\rightarrow\infty}\sup_{\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{Y}}\in\mathcal{S}_{1}(\kappa_{1},M_{1},M_{2})}\mathbb{P}

\displaystyle\left[\|\hat{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{\nu_{n}}-\boldsymbol{\mu}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}>\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\left.D((n\cdot\min(\overline{r},\overline{r}^{\sigma^{2}}))^{-2\kappa_{1}(\frac{2p+q+1}{2p}+2\kappa_{1})^{-1}}+n^{-1})\right]=0.

As mentioned, while setting
\kappa_{1}=0
in
Theorem

7.3
does not yield a meaningful consistency rate at the input level, once we have
\kappa_{1}>0
, the convergence rate is bounded between
(n\cdot\min(\overline{r},\overline{r}^{\sigma^{2}}))^{-\frac{2\kappa_{1}}{2\kappa_{1}+1}}+n^{-1}
and
(n\cdot\min(\overline{r},\overline{r}^{\sigma^{2}}))^{-\frac{2\kappa_{1}}{2\kappa_{1}+1/2}}+n^{-1}
as
2p>q+1
.

Similar to the output-level analysis,
Theorem

7.3
also exhibits a phase transition with respect to the effective tilt numbers, though the boundary now occurs at
n^{(2\kappa_{1})^{-1}(\frac{2p+q+1}{2p})}
. In the sparse tilts regime where
\min(\overline{r},\overline{r}^{\sigma^{2}})=O(n^{(2\kappa_{1})^{-1}(\frac{2p+q+1}{2p})})
, we obtain

\|\hat{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{\nu_{n}}-\boldsymbol{\mu}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}=O_{\mathbb{P}}((n\overline{r})^{-2\kappa_{1}(\frac{2p+q+1}{2p}+2\kappa_{1})^{-1}}),

In the dense tilts regime where
\min(\overline{r},\overline{r}^{\sigma^{2}})\gg n^{(2\kappa_{1})^{-1}(\frac{2p+q+1}{2p})}
, a parametric rate is achieved. The phase transition with respect to heteroscedastic noise is the same as before, with the boundary occurring when the noise function grows linearly.

7.2
Asymptotic Behavior of the Covariance Estimator

As mentioned in
Section

4
, we denote the tensor product between two elements in
\mathbb{H}\otimes\mathbb{H}
by
\otimes_{2}
when we reshuffle the fourth-order tensors. Define the following empirical and population functionals:

\displaystyle\hat{\mathscr{K}}^{\otimes}:=\frac{\omega_{d_{r},d}^{2}}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{r_{i}(r_{i}-1)}\sum_{1\leq j\neq j^{\prime}\leq r_{i}}\frac{1}{s_{ij}s_{ij^{\prime}}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq k\leq s_{ij}\\
1\leq k^{\prime}\leq s_{ij^{\prime}}\end{subarray}}\varphi_{iJJ^{\prime}}^{\otimes}\otimes_{2}\varphi_{iJJ^{\prime}}^{\otimes},

\displaystyle\mathscr{K}^{\otimes}:=\mathbb{E}[\hat{\mathscr{K}}^{\otimes}]=\omega_{d_{r},d}^{2}\mathbb{E}[(\varphi_{ijk}\otimes\varphi_{ij^{\prime}k^{\prime}})\otimes_{2}(\varphi_{ijk}\otimes\varphi_{ij^{\prime}k^{\prime}})]\in\mathcal{B}_{\infty}(\mathbb{H}\otimes\mathbb{H}),\,j\neq j^{\prime}.

(19)

Since
\varphi_{ijk}
and
\varphi_{ij^{\prime}k^{\prime}}
are independent for
j\neq j^{\prime}
, it is important to note that

\displaystyle\mathscr{K}^{\otimes}=\mathscr{K}\otimes\mathscr{K}=\sum_{l,l^{\prime}=1}^{\infty}\tau_{ll^{\prime}}^{2}\sum_{m,m^{\prime}=1}^{N(l)}e^{\otimes}_{lm,l^{\prime}m^{\prime}}\otimes_{2}e^{\otimes}_{lm,l^{\prime}m^{\prime}},

where
\tau_{ll^{\prime}}:=\tau_{l}\tau_{l^{\prime}}
and
e^{\otimes}_{lm,l^{\prime}m^{\prime}}:=e_{lm}\otimes e_{l^{\prime}m^{\prime}}\in\mathbb{H}\otimes\mathbb{H}
forms a CONS for
\mathbb{H}\otimes\mathbb{H}
. Consequently, the s.p.d. operator
\mathscr{K}^{\otimes}
satisfies
\VERT\mathscr{K}^{\otimes}\VERT_{\infty}=\VERT\mathscr{K}\VERT_{\infty}^{2}
and
\VERT(\mathscr{K}^{\otimes})^{\theta}\VERT_{1}=\VERT\mathscr{K}^{2\theta}\VERT_{1}
for
\theta\in(0,\infty)

whenever either side is finite, which is always true for
\theta\geq 1/2
.

For any
j\neq j^{\prime}
, since
\mathbb{E}[\left.Z_{iJJ^{\prime}}\right|\varphi]=\langle\boldsymbol{\Gamma},\varphi_{iJJ^{\prime}}^{\otimes}\rangle_{\mathbb{H}\otimes\mathbb{H}}
by (
9
), we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left(Z_{iJJ^{\prime}}-\langle G,\varphi_{iJJ^{\prime}}^{\otimes}\rangle_{\mathbb{H}\otimes\mathbb{H}}\right)^{2}=\mathbb{E}\left(Z_{iJJ^{\prime}}-\langle\boldsymbol{\Gamma},\varphi_{iJJ^{\prime}}^{\otimes}\rangle_{\mathbb{H}\otimes\mathbb{H}}\right)^{2}+\langle\mathscr{K}^{\otimes}(\boldsymbol{\Gamma}-G),\boldsymbol{\Gamma}-G\rangle_{\mathbb{H}\otimes\mathbb{H}}.

Therefore, the expectation of empirical risk functional in (
5.2
) becomes

\displaystyle L_{\eta}^{\otimes}(G)=\mathbb{E}\left[\left(Z_{iJJ^{\prime}}-\langle\boldsymbol{\Gamma},\varphi_{iJJ^{\prime}}^{\otimes}\rangle_{\mathbb{H}\otimes\mathbb{H}}\right)^{2}\right]+\langle\mathscr{K}^{\otimes}(\boldsymbol{\Gamma}-G),\boldsymbol{\Gamma}-G\rangle_{\mathbb{H}\otimes\mathbb{H}}+\eta\|G\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}\otimes\mathbb{H}}.

Similar to
Section

7.1
, both
\hat{L}_{\eta}^{\odot}
and
L_{\eta}^{\otimes}
admit unique minimizers, given by

\hat{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}_{\eta}:=\argmin_{G\in\mathbb{H}\otimes\mathbb{H}}\hat{L}_{\eta}^{\odot}(G)=(\hat{\mathscr{K}}^{\otimes}+\eta I)^{-1}\mathcal{Z}^{\otimes}_{n},\quad\bar{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}_{\eta}:=\argmin_{G\in\mathbb{H}\otimes\mathbb{H}}L_{\eta}^{\otimes}(G)=(\mathscr{K}^{\otimes}+\nu I)^{-1}\mathscr{K}^{\otimes}\boldsymbol{\Gamma},

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{\otimes}_{n}:=\frac{\omega_{d_{r},d}^{2}}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{r_{i}(r_{i}-1)}\sum_{1\leq j\neq j^{\prime}\leq r_{i}}\frac{1}{s_{ij}s_{ij^{\prime}}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq k\leq s_{ij}\\
1\leq k^{\prime}\leq s_{ij^{\prime}}\end{subarray}}Z_{iJJ^{\prime}}\varphi_{iJJ^{\prime}}^{\otimes}\in\mathbb{H}\otimes\mathbb{H}.

Similar to (
17
), it follows that
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}-\bar{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}_{\eta}=\eta(\mathscr{K}^{\otimes}+\eta I)^{-1}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
.
Following the same reasoning as in
Section

7.1
, we introduce the source regularity condition:

Definition 7.4

(Source regularity)
.

For
0\leq\kappa_{2}\leq 1/2
and
M_{3},M_{4}>0
, we say that a fourth-order
\mathbb{H}
-valued process
\mathcal{Y}
belongs to the class
\mathcal{S}_{2}(\kappa_{2},M_{3},M_{4})
if

\boldsymbol{\Gamma}\in\{(\mathscr{K}^{\otimes})^{\kappa_{2}}\delta^{\otimes}:\delta^{\otimes}\in\mathbb{H}\otimes\mathbb{H},\|\delta^{\otimes}\|_{\mathbb{H}\otimes\mathbb{H}}^{2}\leq M_{3}\},\quad\mathbb{E}[\|\mathcal{Y}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{4}]\leq M_{4}.

When
\kappa_{2}=0
, we simply denote
\mathcal{S}_{2}(\kappa_{2},M_{3},M_{4})
instead of
\mathcal{S}_{2}(M_{3},M_{4})
.

Again, the Douglas factorization lemma
[
19
]
leads to

\boldsymbol{\Gamma}\in\{(\mathscr{K}^{\otimes})^{\kappa_{2}}\delta^{\otimes}:\delta^{\otimes}\in\mathbb{H}\otimes\mathbb{H},\|\delta^{\otimes}\|_{\mathbb{H}\otimes\mathbb{H}}^{2}\leq M_{3}\}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad M_{3}(\mathscr{K}^{\otimes})^{2\kappa_{2}}\succeq\boldsymbol{\Gamma}\otimes_{2}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}.

We formalize the intuition that the source condition for covariance estimation is stronger than that for mean estimation:

Proposition 7.5
.

Let
0\leq\kappa_{2}\leq 1/2
and
M_{3},M_{4}>0
. Then

\mathcal{S}_{2}(\kappa_{2},M_{3},M_{4})\subset\mathcal{S}_{1}(\kappa_{2},\sqrt{M_{3}},\sqrt{M_{4}}).

As in
Section

7.1
, we present our results under the assumption of polynomial decay of the eigenvalues and polynomial growth of their multiplicities, and we refer to
Theorems

C.6
and
C.7
in the Supplement for general results. In contrast to
Section

7.1
, a key difference appears when handling heteroscedastic errors. The rate for the covariance is more sensitive to noise and depends on the
squared-noise-weighted effective tilt number
:

\overline{r}^{\sigma^{4}}:=\left[\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{r_{i}}\left(\frac{\sigma_{r_{i}}^{4}}{\sigma^{4}}\right)\right]^{-1}.

This term, which depends on the fourth power of the noise (
\sigma^{4}
), replaces the
\sigma^{2}
-dependent term used in the mean estimation, signaling a stronger penalty for noisy data. This is because noise is amplified to a greater degree when estimating higher-order moments.

Theorem 7.6
.

Let
M_{3},M_{4}>0
. Assume (A), and suppose that the eigenvalues
\tau_{l}^{2}
of
\mathscr{T}_{\tilde{K}}
satisfies
\tau_{l}\asymp l^{-p}
and the dimension of the corresponding eigenspace
N(l)\asymp l^{q}
for some
p,q\geq 0
with
2p>q+1
. If the penalty parameter satisfies
\eta_{n}\asymp(n\cdot\min(\overline{r},\overline{r}^{\sigma^{4}})/\log n)^{-(\frac{2p+q+1}{2p})^{-1}}
, then

\displaystyle\lim_{D\rightarrow\infty}\limsup_{n\rightarrow\infty}\sup_{\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{Y}}\in\mathcal{S}_{2}(M_{3},M_{4})}\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{Y}}

\displaystyle\left[\|(\mathscr{P}\otimes\mathscr{P})(\hat{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}_{\eta_{n}}-\boldsymbol{\Gamma})\|_{\mathcal{L}_{2}(\mathbb{G}\times\Omega^{d-d_{r}},W_{d_{r},d})}^{2}>\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 60.00009pt\left.D\left(\left(\frac{n\cdot\min(\overline{r},\overline{r}^{\sigma^{4}})}{\log n}\right)^{-\frac{2p}{2p+q+1}}+\frac{1}{n}\right)\right]=0.

Moreover, the covariance estimator
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{\eta_{n}}:=\hat{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}_{\eta_{n}}-\hat{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{\eta_{n}}\otimes\hat{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{\eta_{n}}
achieves the same uniform convergence rate as
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}_{\eta_{n}}
.

In
Theorem

7.6
above, the uniform convergence rate for
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
or
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
in the output
\mathcal{L}_{2}
space, like its counterpart
Theorem

7.2
for the mean, does not require a source regularity parameter (
\kappa_{2}
). However, as in
Theorem

7.3
, the convergence rate of the covariance operator itself in the input space
\mathbb{H}\otimes\mathbb{H}
is not meaningful when
\kappa_{2}=0
:

Theorem 7.7
.

Let
0\leq\kappa_{2}\leq 1/2
and
M_{3},M_{4}>0
. Assume (A), and suppose that the eigenvalues
\tau_{l}^{2}
of
\mathscr{T}_{\tilde{K}}
satisfies
\tau_{l}\asymp l^{-p}
and the dimension of the corresponding eigenspace
N(l)\asymp l^{q}
for some
p,q\geq 0
