with
2p>q+1
. If the penalty parameter satisfies
\eta_{n}\asymp(n\cdot\min(\overline{r},\overline{r}^{\sigma^{4}})/\log n)^{-(\frac{2p+q+1}{2p}+2\kappa_{2})^{-1}}
, then it holds that

\displaystyle\lim_{D\rightarrow\infty}\limsup_{n\rightarrow\infty}\sup_{\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{Y}}\in\mathcal{S}_{2}(\kappa_{2},M_{3},M_{4})}\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{Y}}

\displaystyle\left[\|\hat{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}_{\eta_{n}}-\boldsymbol{\Gamma}\|_{\mathbb{H}\otimes\mathbb{H}}^{2}>\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt\left.D\left(\left(\frac{n\cdot\min(\overline{r},\overline{r}^{\sigma^{4}})}{\log n}\right)^{-2\kappa_{2}(\frac{2p+q+1}{2p}+2\kappa_{2})^{-1}}+\frac{1}{n}\right)\right]=0.

Moreover, the covariance estimator
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{\eta_{n}}:=\hat{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}_{\eta_{n}}-\hat{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{\eta_{n}}\otimes\hat{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{\eta_{n}}
achieves the same uniform convergence rate as
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}_{\eta_{n}}
.

Just as with mean estimation in
Section

7.1
, the asymptotic convergence rate in
Theorems

7.6
and
7.7
for the covariance depends on the effective tilt numbers (
\overline{r},\overline{r}^{\sigma^{4}}
), not on the frequency of sampling at different locations within a projection. While more location samples can lead to sharper non-asymptotic bounds, they do not alter the overall uniform convergence rate.
We again observe a phase transition between sparse and dense sampling regimes:

•

The rate in
Theorem

7.6
is always bounded between
(\min(\overline{r},\overline{r}^{\sigma^{4}})/\log n)^{-1}+n^{-1}
and
(\min(\overline{r},\overline{r}^{\sigma^{4}})/\log n)^{-1/2}+n^{-1}
. As for
Theorem

7.7
, the rate is always bounded between
(\min(\overline{r},\overline{r}^{\sigma^{4}})/\log n)^{-\frac{2\kappa_{2}}{2\kappa_{2}+1}}+n^{-1}
and
(\min(\overline{r},\overline{r}^{\sigma^{4}})/\log n)^{-\frac{2\kappa_{2}}{2\kappa_{2}+1/2}}+n^{-1}
.

•

The critical boundaries where the regime switches from being limited by the effective tilt numbers (sparse tilts) to being limited by the number of particles (dense tilts) are:

\min(\overline{r},\overline{r}^{\sigma^{4}})\asymp n^{\frac{q+1}{2p}}\log n,\quad\min(\overline{r},\overline{r}^{\sigma^{4}})\asymp n^{(2\kappa_{2})^{-1}(\frac{2p+q+1}{2p})}\log n,

for
Theorems

7.6
and
7.7
, respectively.

The most significant difference from the mean estimation is the covariance estimator’s heightened sensitivity to heteroscedastic noise:

•

For the mean estimation in
Theorems

7.2
and
7.3
, the phase transition’s critical boundary was where noise variance
\sigma_{r}^{2}
grew linearly with the number of tilts
r
.

•

For the covariance estimation in
Theorems

7.6
and
7.7
, the boundary occurs when
\sigma_{r}^{2}
grows with the square root of
r
.

This makes covariance estimation far more fragile. If the noise variance per image increases even moderately with the number of acquired tilts (i.e., faster than
\sqrt{r}
), adding more views will actually degrade the quality of the covariance estimate. This imposes a much stricter practical limit on the number of tilts that can be beneficially used.

8
Nuisance Orientations

In cryo-EM, each biomolecule
\mathcal{Y}_{i}
is flash-frozen in a random orientation
\boldsymbol{R}_{i}
, unknown to the experimenter
[
101
]
.
While the microscope applies known, controlled tilts
\tilde{\boldsymbol{R}}_{ij}

(if the imaging was conducted simultaneously across particles, then
\tilde{\boldsymbol{R}}_{ij}\equiv\tilde{\boldsymbol{R}}_{j}
)
during the
j
-th image acquisition, the final viewing orientation is a composite:

\boldsymbol{R}_{ij}=\tilde{\boldsymbol{R}}_{ij}\boldsymbol{R}_{i}

(20)

Because the initial orientation
\boldsymbol{R}_{i}
is a latent variable, the absolute first and second moments of the particles are unidentifiable.
Fortunately, key structural features of biomolecules – such as the icosahedral symmetry of spherical viruses
[
119
]
, the pseudo-twofold symmetry of nucleosomes
[
62
]
, and the tetrameric arrangement of aquaporins
[
67
]
– are preserved under random orientations. Consequently, it is both reasonable and biologically motivated to focus on
\mathbb{G}
-invariant statistics. Treating the initial orientations as nuisance parameters, we formally define these orientationally averaged quantities as:

\displaystyle\overline{\boldsymbol{\mu}}:=\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{R}}[\rho(\boldsymbol{R})\boldsymbol{\mu}]\in\mathbb{H},\quad\overline{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}:=\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{R}}[\rho(\boldsymbol{R})\boldsymbol{\Gamma}\rho(\boldsymbol{R}^{-1})]\in\mathbb{H}\otimes\mathbb{H},

\displaystyle\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}:=\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{R}}[\rho(\boldsymbol{R})\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\rho(\boldsymbol{R}^{-1})]=\overline{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}-\overline{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\otimes\overline{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\in\mathbb{H}\otimes\mathbb{H},

where
\boldsymbol{R}\sim\mathbb{P}_{\boldsymbol{R}}
is a uniform distribution on
\mathbb{G}

[
51
,
22
]
. By construction, these averaged statistics are also
\mathbb{G}
-invariant:

\overline{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\boldsymbol{z})=\overline{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(R\boldsymbol{z}),\quad\overline{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}(\boldsymbol{z},\boldsymbol{z}^{\prime})=\overline{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}(R\boldsymbol{z},R\boldsymbol{z}^{\prime}),\quad\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}(\boldsymbol{z},\boldsymbol{z}^{\prime})=\overline{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}(R\boldsymbol{z},R\boldsymbol{z}^{\prime}),\quad R\in\mathbb{G},\,\boldsymbol{z},\boldsymbol{z}^{\prime}\in\Omega^{d}.

From an identifiability perspective, adopting this framework is statistically equivalent to assuming the random process
\mathcal{Y}
itself is
\mathbb{G}
-invariant, i.e.,
\mathcal{Y}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\equiv}}\rho(R)\mathcal{Y}
for any
R\in\mathbb{G}
. This assumption simplifies the estimation procedure significantly: the specific unknown orientations
\boldsymbol{R}_{i}

become irrelevant, and all that matters are the known, relative orientations
\tilde{\boldsymbol{R}}_{ij}
between the tilted images.

To proceed with estimation, we define
\mathbb{G}
-invariant feature maps and their tensor products by averaging over the orientations:

\displaystyle\overline{\varphi}:

\displaystyle\mathbb{G}\times\Omega^{d-d_{r}}\rightarrow\mathbb{H},(R,\boldsymbol{x})\mapsto\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{R}}[\rho(\boldsymbol{R})\varphi(R,\boldsymbol{x})],

\displaystyle\overline{\varphi\otimes\varphi}:

\displaystyle(\mathbb{G}\times\Omega^{d-d_{r}})\times(\mathbb{G}\times\Omega^{d-d_{r}})\rightarrow\mathbb{H}\otimes\mathbb{H},

\displaystyle((R,\boldsymbol{x}),(R^{\prime},\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}))\mapsto\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{R}}[\rho(\boldsymbol{R})\circ(\varphi(R,\boldsymbol{x})\otimes\varphi(R^{\prime},\boldsymbol{x}))\circ\rho(\boldsymbol{R}^{-1})],

which inherits
\mathbb{G}
-invariance from the Haar measure:

\overline{\varphi}(R,\boldsymbol{x})=\overline{\varphi}(I,\boldsymbol{x}),\quad\overline{\varphi\otimes\varphi}(R,\boldsymbol{x},R^{\prime},\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime})=\overline{\varphi\otimes\varphi}(R(R^{\prime})^{-1},\boldsymbol{x},I,\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}).

Hence, we simply denote
\overline{\varphi}(\boldsymbol{x})
and
\overline{\varphi\otimes\varphi}(R,\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime})
, instead of
\overline{\varphi}(R,\boldsymbol{x})
and
\overline{\varphi\otimes\varphi}(R,\boldsymbol{x},I,\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime})
, respectively.

Remark 8.1
.

Recall from
Proposition

A.6
that
\varphi(R,\boldsymbol{x})(\boldsymbol{z})=\mathscr{P}_{R}k_{\boldsymbol{z}}(\boldsymbol{x})
, hence

\overline{\varphi}(\boldsymbol{x})(\boldsymbol{z})=\int_{\mathbb{G}}\varphi(R,\boldsymbol{x})(\boldsymbol{z})\mathrm{d}R=\int_{\mathbb{G}}\mathscr{P}_{R}k_{\boldsymbol{z}}(\boldsymbol{x})\mathrm{d}R=\left[\mathscr{P}_{I}\left(\int_{\mathbb{G}}k_{R\boldsymbol{z}}\mathrm{d}R\right)\right](\boldsymbol{x}).

For the X-ray transform, this can be computed explicitly, see Proposition B.2. in
[
117
]
:

\displaystyle\overline{\varphi}(\boldsymbol{x})(\boldsymbol{z})=\frac{1}{|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|}\int_{\mathbb{B}^{d}\backslash\|\boldsymbol{x}\|\cdot\mathbb{B}^{d}}\frac{K(\boldsymbol{z},\boldsymbol{z}^{\prime})}{\|\boldsymbol{z}^{\prime}\|^{d-2}(\|\boldsymbol{z}^{\prime}\|-\|\boldsymbol{x}\|^{2})^{1/2}}\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{z}^{\prime},

which only depends on
\|\boldsymbol{x}\|
and
\|\boldsymbol{z}\|
.

Crucially, even though
\boldsymbol{R}_{i}\in\mathbb{G}
are unknown, these
\mathbb{G}
-invariant feature maps are still computable random elements from the known experimental parameters
\tilde{\boldsymbol{R}}_{ij}
and
\boldsymbol{X}_{ijk}
:

\displaystyle\overline{\varphi}_{ijk}:=\overline{\varphi}(\boldsymbol{R}_{ij},\boldsymbol{X}_{ijk})=\overline{\varphi}(\boldsymbol{X}_{ijk}),

\displaystyle\overline{\varphi}_{iJJ^{\prime}}^{\otimes}:=\overline{\varphi\otimes\varphi}(\boldsymbol{R}_{ij},\boldsymbol{X}_{ijk},\boldsymbol{R}_{ij^{\prime}},\boldsymbol{X}_{ij^{\prime}k^{\prime}})=\overline{\varphi\otimes\varphi}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{R}}_{ij},\boldsymbol{X}_{ijk},\tilde{\boldsymbol{R}}_{ij^{\prime}},\boldsymbol{X}_{ij^{\prime}k^{\prime}}).

Proposition 8.2
.

For the observation model in (
7
) with nuisance orientations from (
20
), the conditional expectations are given by:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[Z_{ijk}|(\tilde{\boldsymbol{R}}_{ij},\boldsymbol{X}_{ijk})]=\langle\boldsymbol{\mu},\overline{\varphi}_{ijk}\rangle_{\mathbb{H}},

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[Z_{ijk}Z_{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}k^{\prime}}|(\tilde{\boldsymbol{R}}_{ij},\boldsymbol{X}_{ijk}),(\tilde{\boldsymbol{R}}_{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}},\boldsymbol{X}_{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}k^{\prime}})]=\begin{cases}\langle\boldsymbol{\mu},\overline{\varphi}_{ijk}\rangle_{\mathbb{H}}\langle\boldsymbol{\mu},\overline{\varphi}_{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}k^{\prime}}\rangle_{\mathbb{H}},&i\neq i^{\prime},\\
\langle\boldsymbol{\Gamma},\overline{\varphi}_{iJJ^{\prime}}^{\otimes}\rangle_{\mathbb{H}\otimes\mathbb{H}}+\sigma_{r_{i}}^{2}\delta_{jj^{\prime}}\delta_{kk^{\prime}},&i=i^{\prime}.\end{cases}

(21)

Additionally, for any
\boldsymbol{\mu}\in\mathbb{H}
and
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}\in\mathbb{H}\otimes\mathbb{H}
, the inner products are equivalent to those with their
\mathbb{G}
-invariant counterparts:

\langle\boldsymbol{\mu},\overline{\varphi}_{ijk}\rangle_{\mathbb{H}}=\langle\overline{\boldsymbol{\mu}},\overline{\varphi}_{ijk}\rangle_{\mathbb{H}},\quad\langle\boldsymbol{\Gamma},\overline{\varphi}_{iJJ^{\prime}}^{\otimes}\rangle_{\mathbb{H}\otimes\mathbb{H}}=\langle\overline{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}},\overline{\varphi}_{iJJ^{\prime}}^{\otimes}\rangle_{\mathbb{H}\otimes\mathbb{H}}.

Similar to the standard case, if we define

\displaystyle\overline{L}_{\nu}(f)=\frac{\omega_{d_{r},d}}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{r_{i}}\sum_{j=1}^{r_{i}}\frac{1}{s_{ij}}\sum_{k=1}^{s_{ij}}\left(Z_{ijk}-\langle f,\overline{\varphi}_{ijk}\rangle_{\mathbb{H}}\right)^{2}+\nu\|f\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}},

we recover the representer theorem to estimate
\overline{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
:

\displaystyle\argmin_{f\in\mathbb{H}}\overline{L}_{\nu}(f)\in\text{span}\{\overline{\varphi}_{ijk}:1\leq i\leq n,1\leq j\leq r_{i},1\leq k\leq s_{ij}\},

which is guaranteed to be a
\mathbb{G}
-invariant function. The estimator’s coefficients are found by solving a linear system with a modified Gram matrix, by replacing
\varphi_{ijk}
with
\overline{\varphi}_{ijk}
in (
11
):

\overline{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}:=\left[\overline{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{i_{1}j_{1}k_{1},i_{2}j_{2}k_{2}}:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{r_{i_{1}}s_{i_{1}j_{1}}}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{r_{i_{2}}s_{i_{2}j_{2}}}}\langle\overline{\varphi}_{i_{1}j_{1}k_{1}},\overline{\varphi}_{i_{2}j_{2}k_{2}}\rangle_{\mathbb{H}}\right].

The estimation of the second moment
\overline{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}
follows the exact same logic, replacing the corresponding quantities in (
12
) with their
\mathbb{G}
-invariant versions, so we omit the detailed formulation.
Because this
\mathbb{G}
-invariant framework depends only on relative orientations, consistent estimation is feasible, and all asymptotic results from
Section

7
apply directly.

9
Simulation Study

We set
d=2
and generate
n=100
random functions. To address low-dose tomography setups
[
109
,
88
,
18
,
112
,
99
]
, we sample
r=5
angles for each function, and
s=10
locations for each angle.
In homogeneous inverse problems, the 2D Shepp-Logan phantom – formed by the overlap of several ellipses – serves as a standard benchmark image for tomographic simulations. Analogously, we generate random functions by superimposing several fixed ellipsoidal-shaped bump functions, each modulated by a random intensity, as illustrated in
Fig.

2
. Specifically, let
Q=6
denote the number of bump functions, each characterized by a location parameter
\boldsymbol{l}_{q}\in\mathbb{B}^{2}
, and a dispersion matrix
\boldsymbol{S}_{q}\in\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}
. We then define
n
realizations of random functions as:

\mathcal{Y}_{i}(\boldsymbol{z})=\sum_{q=1}^{Q}\xi_{q}^{i}\Psi[(\boldsymbol{z}-\boldsymbol{l}_{q})^{\top}\boldsymbol{S}_{q}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{z}-\boldsymbol{l}_{q})],\quad\boldsymbol{z}\in\mathbb{B}^{2},\,1\leq i\leq n,

where
\Psi
is a compactly supported radial function

\Psi(r)=\frac{\tanh(5(1-r))^{2}}{\tanh(5(1-r))^{2}+\tanh(5r)^{2}}\cdot I(r\leq 1).

For intensity, the i.i.d. random coefficients
\boldsymbol{\xi}^{i}=(\xi_{1}^{i},\dots,\xi_{Q}^{i})^{\top}
are drawn from a multivariate normal distribution
\mathcal{N}_{Q}(\boldsymbol{m},\boldsymbol{C})
with mean vector
\boldsymbol{m}\in\mathbb{R}^{Q}
and covariance matrix
\boldsymbol{C}\in\mathbb{R}^{Q\times Q}
.
Consequently, the mean and covariance functions are given by

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}(\boldsymbol{z})=\sum_{q=1}^{Q}\boldsymbol{m}_{q}\Psi[(\boldsymbol{z}-\boldsymbol{l}_{q})^{\top}\boldsymbol{S}_{q}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{z}-\boldsymbol{l}_{q})],\quad\boldsymbol{z}\in\mathbb{B}^{2},

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Sigma}(\boldsymbol{z},\boldsymbol{z}^{\prime})=\sum_{q,q^{\prime}=1}^{Q}\boldsymbol{C}_{qq^{\prime}}\Psi[(\boldsymbol{z}-\boldsymbol{l}_{q})^{\top}\boldsymbol{S}_{q}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{z}-\boldsymbol{l}_{q})]\Psi[(\boldsymbol{z}^{\prime}-\boldsymbol{l}_{q^{\prime}})^{\top}\boldsymbol{S}_{q^{\prime}}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{z}^{\prime}-\boldsymbol{l}_{q^{\prime}})],\quad\boldsymbol{z},\boldsymbol{z}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{B}^{2}.

Fig 2:
Six realizations out of random phantoms, composed of ellipsoidal bump functions with random intensities.

Since
d=2
, the
k
-plane, Radon, and X-ray transforms all coincide, which is the forward operator used to generate the observed data in
Fig.

3
, where we take the sampling distributions
\mathbb{P}_{\boldsymbol{R}}
and
\mathbb{P}_{\boldsymbol{X}}
to be uniform on
SO(2)
and
[-1,+1]
, respectively, with the noise level
\sigma=0.1
.
In two dimensions,
SO(2)
is isomorphic to the circle
\mathbb{R}/(2\pi\mathbb{Z})
:

\phi\in\mathbb{R}/(2\pi\mathbb{Z})\ \cong\ \boldsymbol{E}(\phi)=\begin{pmatrix}\cos\phi&-\sin\phi\\
\sin\phi&\cos\phi\end{pmatrix}\in SO(2).

Since the transform satisfies
antipodal
symmetry, i.e.,
\mathscr{P}f(\boldsymbol{E}(\phi),x)=\mathscr{P}f(\boldsymbol{E}(\phi+\pi),-x)
, instead of sampling from
\mathbb{P}_{\boldsymbol{R}}
, we equivalently sample
\phi\sim\mathrm{Unif}[0,\pi)
, which avoids redundancy in data acquisition.

Fig 3:
The mean function
\boldsymbol{\mu}
is depicted on the left panel, with the corresponding sinogram
\mathscr{P}\boldsymbol{\mu}
is shown on the right panel in grayscale. Overlaid are colored dots representing i.i.d. pairs of viewing angle and positions
(\boldsymbol{\phi}_{ij},\boldsymbol{X}_{ijk})\sim\mathrm{Unif}([0,\pi)\times[-1,+1])
, with seven different colors indicating different random functions.

For the estimation, we employ the Gaussian kernel

K_{\gamma}(\boldsymbol{z}_{1},\boldsymbol{z}_{2})=\exp(-\gamma\|\boldsymbol{z}_{1}-\boldsymbol{z}_{2}\|^{2}),\quad\gamma>0,\,\boldsymbol{z}_{1},\boldsymbol{z}_{2}\in\mathbb{B}^{2}.

with bandwidth parameter
\gamma=2^{8}
. The induced kernel
\tilde{K}
used to construct the mean Gram matrix
\boldsymbol{\Phi}
can be computed explicitly; see
[
117
]
for details. For both the mean and second-moment estimations shown in
Fig.

4
, we set the regularization parameters to
\nu=\eta=2^{-8}
for the representer theorem in
Theorem

5.4
, using the implementation detailed in
Section

B
.

Fig 4:
Top row: the ground truth and estimated mean function
\boldsymbol{\mu}(\boldsymbol{z})
. Bottom row: the diagonal of the second-moment function
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}(\boldsymbol{z},\boldsymbol{z})
, for
\boldsymbol{z}\in\mathbb{B}^{2}
.
Left column: ground truth. Middle column: estimators
\hat{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{\nu}(\boldsymbol{z})
and
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}_{\eta}(\boldsymbol{z},\boldsymbol{z})
computed using the Gaussian kernel
K_{\gamma}
. Right column: pointwise absolute error between the estimates and ground truth, with reported Root Mean Squared Error (RMSE) for quantitative comparison.

After obtaining
\hat{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{\nu}
and
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}_{\eta}
, we perform kernel PCA using
Theorem

B.2
:

\hat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{\nu,\eta}=\hat{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}_{\eta}-\hat{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{\nu}\otimes\hat{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{\nu}=\sum_{l=1}^{\mathrm{rank}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{\nu,\eta})}\hat{\lambda}_{l}\ \hat{\psi}_{l}\otimes\hat{\psi}_{l}\in\mathbb{H}\otimes\mathbb{H},

where each principal component
\hat{\psi}_{l}\in\mathbb{H}
is an orthonormal eigenfunction corresponding to the eigenvalue
\hat{\lambda}_{l}\in\mathbb{R}
. The results are illustrated in
Fig.

5
, where we observe that the first principal component effectively captures the dominant covariance structure in our simulation.

Fig 5:
Top panel: Scree plot of the first 20 eigenvalues of the estimated covariance operator. A clear spectral gap is observed after the leading eigenvalue, indicating that the first principal component dominates the variability. Bottom panels: The first three principal components of the covariance operator.

10
Conclusion

Our work combines methods from inverse problems
[
36
,
48
,
21
]
and FDA
[
42
,
85
]
to provide statistical foundations for the central challenge of continuous heterogeneity. Our framework provides a consistent, theoretically-grounded solution by using the representer theorems in a RKHS setup, which reduces the problem to a finite-dimensional regression made practical by our efficient
TReK
algorithm to overcome the limitations of traditional FBP
[
70
]
. By enabling the principled estimation of covariance, our method facilitates the quantification of conformational variability from pooled data, making it especially valuable in low-dose, high-noise imaging regimes. The framework encompasses several tomographic modalities – including electron, photoacoustic, and optical tomography
[
109
,
88
,
18
,
112
,
99
]
– and is supported by rigorous asymptotic guarantees.

Supplementary Material

A. Sinogram Operators

It shows how our theoretical results, based on orientational duality, extend beyond the X-ray transform to general linear inverse problems.

B. Implementation

It provides detailed implementation notes for our estimators. It focuses on the Krylov methods used to efficiently solve the large linear system arising from the covariance estimation.

C. Proofs

It contains the detailed mathematical proofs for all
theorems, propositions, and lemmas
.

D. Index

It provides a comprehensive
list of notations
, serving as a quick reference.

Supplementary Material

A
Sinogram Operators

Throughout the Supplement, we consider our problem setup and the associated proofs in a more general setup. First, we let an input
f
to be a
d
-dimensional functions defined on a shared support
\Omega^{d}
, a compact subset of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and output
g
as
(d-d_{r})
-dimensional functions defined on a shared compact support
\Omega^{d-d_{r}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d-d_{r}}
, where
0\leq d_{r}\leq d
. For simplicity, we consider the Lie group
\mathbb{G}
to be compact, so that
\mathbb{G}
ensures the uniqueness of the normalized Haar measure. Additionally, we conceptualize the set of orientations as a Lie group acting on
\Omega^{d}
from the left, denoted as
\mathbb{G}
. We denote the left action of
R\in\mathbb{G}
by
\rho(R):\Omega^{d}\rightarrow\Omega^{d},\boldsymbol{z}\mapsto R\boldsymbol{z}
. The forward operator
\mathscr{P}_{R}
at orientation
R\in\mathbb{G}
generates a
(d-d_{r})
-dimensional projection image of the
d
-dimensional input function.

We present what type of forward operators
\{\mathscr{P}_{R}:R\in\mathbb{G}\}
across orientations are considered in the context of inverse problems.
While the forward operator
f\mapsto\mathscr{P}_{R}f
could be well-defined on different domains or ranges, the analytical properties of the operator
\mathscr{P}_{R}
depend on these choices, affecting the quality of reconstructed image
[
36
,
117
]
. As a starting point, we first assume that
\mathscr{P}_{R}
is a well-defined continuous linear operator between some weighted
\mathcal{L}_{2}
spaces for any orientation
R\in\mathbb{G}
. Then, we restrict the domain by an RKHS, and discuss how this alteration changes the properties of the forward operator.
Reliable reconstruction necessitates sectional images from multiple orientations, and they should be systematically intertwined, leading to the following definition:

Definition A.1

(Sinogram Operator)
.

A collection of forward operators
\mathscr{P}=\{\mathscr{P}_{R}:R\in\mathbb{G}\}
is said to be a
sinogram
operator if

1.

\mathscr{P}_{I}\in\mathcal{B}_{\infty}(\mathcal{L}_{2}(\Omega^{d},W_{0,d}),\mathcal{L}_{2}(\Omega^{d-d_{r}},W_{d_{r},d}))
is a continuous linear operator.

2.

If
f:\Omega^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is a continuous function, then
\mathscr{P}_{I}f:\Omega^{d-d_{r}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is also a continuous function.

3.

For any
R\in\mathbb{G}
, it holds that
\mathscr{P}_{R}=\mathscr{P}_{I}\circ\rho(R)
.

In this case, we identify the sinogram operator
\mathscr{P}:\mathcal{L}_{2}(\Omega^{d},W_{0,d})\rightarrow\mathcal{L}_{2}(\mathbb{G}\times\Omega^{d-d_{r}},W_{d_{r},d}))
through
\mathscr{P}f(R,\boldsymbol{x}):=\mathscr{P}_{R}f(\boldsymbol{x})
.

\mathscr{P}_{R}=\mathscr{P}_{I}\circ\rho(R)
dictates the dual perspective—rotating the viewing orientation is equivalent to rotating the input function in the opposite direction. This leads to
\mathscr{P}^{*}_{R}=\rho(R^{-1})\circ\mathscr{P}^{*}_{I}
and that the sinogram operator
\mathscr{P}
is continuous, with its adjoint given by

\mathscr{P}^{*}g=\int_{\mathbb{G}}\mathscr{P}^{*}_{R}g(R,\cdot)\mathrm{d}R,\quad g\in\mathcal{L}_{2}(\mathbb{G}\times\Omega^{d-d_{r}},W_{d_{r},d}),

in the sense of Bochner integration
[
16
,
15
]
.

Remark A.2
.

The
k
-plane transform is a special case of
Definition

A.1
with
\mathbb{G}=SO(d)
,
\Omega^{d}=\mathbb{B}^{d}
,
\Omega^{d-d_{r}}=\mathbb{B}^{d-d_{r}}
, and the weight functions
W_{0,d}=1
and
W_{d_{r},d}=(1-\|x\|^{2})^{-d_{r}/2}
.
However, if we let
\Omega^{d}=\mathbb{R}^{d}
, then
\mathscr{P}_{I}
is no longer continuous
[
70
]
, which shows that the compactness of
\Omega^{d}
is necessary. Later on, the compactness of
\Omega^{d}
ensures the Mercer decomposition in
Section

6
.

We next explain how the Lie group
\mathbb{G}
induces unitary representations on the input and output function spaces, and how these are connected through duality.

Definition A.3

(Representation)
.

Let
\mathbb{G}
be a Lie group, and
\mathcal{H}
be a Hilbert space. A representation
\rho
of
\mathbb{G}
on
\mathcal{H}
is a map
\rho:\mathbb{G}\rightarrow\mathcal{B}_{\infty}(\mathcal{H}),R\mapsto\rho(R)
such that

1.

\rho(I)
is the identity map and
\rho(R_{1}R_{2})=\rho(R_{1})\rho(R_{2})
for any
R_{1},R_{2}\in\mathbb{G}
.

2.

(strong operator topology (SOT)) For any
f\in\mathcal{H}
, the map
\mathbb{G}\rightarrow\mathcal{H},R\mapsto\rho(R)f
is continuous.

\rho
is said to be
unitary
if
\|\rho(R)f\|_{\mathcal{H}}=\|f\|_{\mathcal{H}}
for any
f\in\mathcal{H}
and
R\in\mathbb{G}
.
A subspace
\mathcal{H}_{0}\subset\mathcal{H}
is said to be
\rho
-invariant if
\rho(R)\mathcal{H}_{0}=\mathcal{H}_{0}
for any
R\in\mathbb{G}
.
Given two representations
(\rho_{1},\mathcal{H}_{1})
and
(\rho_{2},\mathcal{H}_{2})
of
\mathbb{G}
, a continuous linear operator
\mathscr{P}\in\mathcal{B}_{\infty}(\mathcal{H}_{1},\mathcal{H}_{2})
is said to be
intertwining
if
\mathscr{P}\circ\rho_{1}(R)=\rho_{2}(R)\circ\mathscr{P}
for any
R\in\mathbb{G}
.

If
\dim\mathcal{H}=\infty
, then the Banach space
\mathcal{B}_{\infty}(\mathcal{H})
equipped with the norm topology is not separable
[
16
]
, hence the SOT is a suitable topology to work with the representation theory, which is weaker than the norm topology
[
22
,
108
]
.

Proposition A.4
.

Let
(\rho,\mathcal{L}_{2}(\Omega^{d},W_{0,d}))
and
(\rho,\mathcal{L}_{2}(\mathbb{G}\times\Omega^{d-d_{r}},W_{d_{r},d}))
be defined as in (
3
) and (
4
), respectively. Then, they are both unitary representations of
\mathbb{G}
. Moreover, the sinogram operator
\mathscr{P}:\mathcal{L}_{2}(\Omega^{d},W_{0,d})\rightarrow\mathcal{L}_{2}(\mathbb{G}\times\Omega^{d-d_{r}},W_{d_{r},d})
is an intertwining operator.

Proof.

Denote by
\mathcal{H}_{1}=\mathcal{L}_{2}(\Omega^{d},W_{0,d})
and
\mathcal{H}_{2}=\mathcal{L}_{2}(\mathbb{G}\times\Omega^{d-d_{r}},W_{d_{r},d})
. For
\rho:\mathbb{G}\rightarrow\mathcal{B}_{\infty}(\mathcal{H}_{1})
given in (
3
), it is clear that
\rho(I)
is the identity map and
\rho(R_{1}R_{2})=\rho(R_{1})\rho(R_{2})
for any
R_{1},R_{2}\in\mathbb{G}
. Also, they are unitary since the weight
W_{0,d}
is
\mathbb{G}
-invariant. Therefore, it remains to show that
\rho:\mathbb{G}\rightarrow\mathcal{B}_{\infty}(\mathcal{H}_{1})
is continuous at
I\in\mathbb{G}
with respect to the strong operator topology. Let
f\in\mathcal{H}_{1}
and
\varepsilon>0
be given. Since
C(\Omega^{d})
is dense in
\mathcal{H}_{1}
by the Stone-Weierstrasse theorem, there is some
\tilde{f}\in C(\Omega^{d})
such that
\|f-\tilde{f}\|_{\mathcal{H}}<\varepsilon
. Also,
\tilde{f}
is uniformly continuous as
\Omega^{d}
is compact, so there exists some
\delta>0
such that
\|R-I\|<\delta
implies

\sup_{\boldsymbol{z}\in\Omega^{d}}\left|\tilde{f}(R^{-1}\boldsymbol{z})-\tilde{f}(\boldsymbol{z})\right|<\varepsilon.

Thus, whenever
\|R-I\|<\delta
,

\displaystyle\|\rho(R)\tilde{f}-\tilde{f}\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}=\int_{\Omega^{d}}[\tilde{f}(R^{-1}\boldsymbol{z})-\tilde{f}(\boldsymbol{z})]^{2}W_{0,d}(\boldsymbol{z})\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{z}\leq\omega_{0,d}\cdot\varepsilon^{2},

where
\omega_{0,d}
is defined in (
5
), which leads to

\|\rho(R)f-f\|_{\mathcal{H}}\leq\|\rho(R)(f-\tilde{f})\|_{\mathcal{H}}+\|\rho(R)\tilde{f}-\tilde{f}\|_{\mathcal{H}}+\|\tilde{f}-f\|_{\mathcal{H}}<\varepsilon(2+\sqrt{\omega_{0,d}}).

This demonstrates that
\rho:\mathbb{G}\rightarrow\mathcal{B}_{\infty}(\mathcal{H}_{1})
is a unitary representation, and one can also prove in the same way that
\rho:\mathbb{G}\rightarrow\mathcal{B}_{\infty}(\mathcal{H}_{2})
is also a unitary representation. Finally, for any
f\in\mathcal{H}_{1}
, we have

\displaystyle[\mathscr{P}\rho(R_{1})f](R_{2},\cdot)

\displaystyle=\mathscr{P}_{R_{2}}\circ\rho(R_{1})f=\mathscr{P}_{I}\rho(R_{2})\rho(R_{1})f=\mathscr{P}_{I}\rho(R_{2}R_{1})f

\displaystyle=\mathscr{P}_{R_{2}R_{1}}f=\mathscr{P}f(R_{2}R_{1},\cdot)=[\rho(R_{1})\mathscr{P}f](R_{2},\cdot),

thus
\mathscr{P}:\mathcal{H}_{1}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}_{2}
is intertwining.
∎

Even when the sinogram operator
\mathscr{P}
is injective,
\mathscr{P}^{\dagger}
is unbounded unless
\mathcal{R}(\mathscr{P})
is closed in
\mathcal{L}_{2}(\mathbb{G}\times\Omega^{d-d_{r}},W_{d_{r},d})
. This implies that even a small perturbation could have a fatal impact on our reconstruction. The
k
-plane transform is compact with infinite-dimensional range, hence
\mathscr{P}^{\dagger}
is necessarily unbounded
[
15
]
.
Moreover, with only finitely many orientations
R_{1},\dots,R_{r}\in\mathbb{G}
, it is often the case that
\cap_{j=1}^{r}\mathcal{N}(\mathscr{P}_{R_{j}})\neq\{0\}
—as is true for the
k
-plane transform
[
70
]
. This underscores the inherent ill-posedness: reconstruction from limited views admits infinitely many solutions
[
36
,
33
,
69
]
. Regularization techniques, such as truncated SVD
[
43
,
63
]
, are therefore essential. Since the sinogram operator
\mathscr{P}
leads to an ill-posed inverse problem, we seek kernels that induce a reproducing property compatible with
\mathscr{P}
.

Definition A.5

(Sinogram Kernel)
.

Let
\mathscr{P}
be a sinogram operator as in
Definition

A.1
. A Mercer kernel
K:\Omega^{d}\times\Omega^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
on
\Omega^{d}
is said to be a sinogram kernel if

1.

K
is
\mathbb{G}
-invariant, i.e.

K(\boldsymbol{z}_{1},\boldsymbol{z}_{2})=K(R\boldsymbol{z}_{1},R\boldsymbol{z}_{2}),\quad R\in\mathbb{G},\boldsymbol{z}_{1},\boldsymbol{z}_{2}\in\Omega^{d}.

(S.1)

2.

At orientation
I\in\mathbb{G}
, there is some constant
B_{\boldsymbol{x}}>0
for every
\boldsymbol{x}\in\Omega^{d-d_{r}}
such that

|\mathscr{P}_{I}f(\boldsymbol{x})|\leq B_{\boldsymbol{x}}\|f\|_{\mathbb{H}},\quad f\in\mathbb{H}(K).

(S.2)

Note that (
S.1
) is equivalent to
\rho(R)k_{\boldsymbol{z}}=K(\boldsymbol{z},R^{-1}\cdot)=K(R\boldsymbol{z},\cdot)=k_{R\boldsymbol{z}}
,
thus
(\rho,\mathbb{H})
is a unitary representation of
\mathbb{G}
. Indeed, (
S.2
) holds for any orientation
R\in\mathbb{G}
:

|\mathscr{P}_{R}f(\boldsymbol{x})|=|\mathscr{P}_{I}[\rho(R)f](\boldsymbol{x})|\leq B_{\boldsymbol{x}}\|\rho(R)f\|_{\mathbb{H}}=B_{\boldsymbol{x}}\|f\|_{\mathbb{H}},\quad f\in\mathbb{H}.

(S.3)

Due to the Riesz representation theorem, (
S.3
) is equivalent to the fact that,
for each
R\in\mathbb{G}
and
\boldsymbol{x}\in\Omega^{d-d_{r}}
, there is some
\varphi(R,\boldsymbol{x})\in\mathbb{H}
with
\|\varphi(R,\boldsymbol{x})\|_{\mathbb{H}}\leq B_{\boldsymbol{x}}
with

\langle\varphi(R,\boldsymbol{x}),f\rangle_{\mathbb{H}}=\mathscr{P}f(R,\boldsymbol{x}),\quad f\in\mathbb{H}.

(S.4)

Using the unitary representation
(\rho,\mathbb{H})
, it is immediate that

\varphi(R,\boldsymbol{x})=\rho(R^{-1})\varphi(I,\boldsymbol{x}),\quad R\in\mathbb{G},\boldsymbol{x}\in\Omega^{d-d_{r}}.

(S.5)

Since
K
is a Mercer kernel, the feature map
\varphi(R,\boldsymbol{x})\in\mathbb{H}(K)
is a continuous function. The following proposition offers a description how to evaluate this map, which is a fundamental building block to obtain representer theorems in
Section

5.2
:

Proposition A.6
.

Let
\mathscr{P}
be a sinogram operator and
K:\Omega^{d}\times\Omega^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be a sinogram kernel as in
Definition

A.5
.
Then
\varphi(R,\boldsymbol{x}):\Omega^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
in (
S.4
) is characterized by

\varphi(R,\boldsymbol{x})(\boldsymbol{z})=\mathscr{P}_{R}k_{\boldsymbol{z}}(\boldsymbol{x}),\quad\boldsymbol{x}\in\Omega^{d-d_{r}},\boldsymbol{z}\in\Omega^{d},R\in\mathbb{G},

and for any
R,R^{\prime}\in\mathbb{G}
and
\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}\in\Omega^{d-d_{r}}
,

\langle\varphi(R,\boldsymbol{x}),\varphi(R^{\prime},\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime})\rangle_{\mathbb{H}}=\langle\varphi(I,\boldsymbol{x}),\varphi(R^{\prime}R^{-1},\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime})\rangle_{\mathbb{H}}=[\mathscr{P}_{I}\varphi(R^{\prime}R^{-1},\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime})](\boldsymbol{x}).

(S.6)

Additionally,
\varphi:\mathbb{G}\times\Omega^{d-d_{r}}\rightarrow\mathbb{H},(R,\boldsymbol{x})\mapsto\varphi(R,\boldsymbol{x})
is a continuous mapping.

Proof.

First, note that

\varphi(R,\boldsymbol{x})(\boldsymbol{z})=\langle\varphi(R,\boldsymbol{x}),k_{\boldsymbol{z}}\rangle_{\mathbb{H}}=\mathscr{P}k_{\boldsymbol{z}}(R,\boldsymbol{x})=\mathscr{P}_{R}k_{\boldsymbol{z}}(\boldsymbol{x}),\quad\boldsymbol{x}\in\Omega^{d-d_{r}},\boldsymbol{z}\in\Omega^{d},R\in\mathbb{G}.

Additionally, due to (
S.5
), we have

\langle\varphi(R,\boldsymbol{x}),\varphi(R^{\prime},\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime})\rangle_{\mathbb{H}}=\langle\varphi(I,\boldsymbol{x}),\varphi(R^{\prime}R^{-1},\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime})\rangle_{\mathbb{H}}=[\mathscr{P}_{I}\varphi(R^{\prime}R^{-1},\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime})](\boldsymbol{x}).

To show that
\varphi:\mathbb{G}\times\Omega^{d-d_{r}}\rightarrow\mathbb{H},(R,\boldsymbol{x})\mapsto\varphi(R,\boldsymbol{x})
is continuous, it suffices to show the continuity with
R=I
fixed due to
Proposition

A.4
. For
\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}\in\Omega^{d-d_{r}}
,

\displaystyle\|\varphi(I,\boldsymbol{x})-\varphi(I,\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime})\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}=\left([\mathscr{P}\varphi(I,\boldsymbol{x})](I,\boldsymbol{x})-[\mathscr{P}\varphi(I,\boldsymbol{x})](I,\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime})\right)-\left([\mathscr{P}\varphi(I,\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime})](I,\boldsymbol{x})-[\mathscr{P}\varphi(I,\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime})](I,\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime})\right).

Since
\varphi(I,\boldsymbol{x}),\varphi(I,\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime})
are continuous functions, both
\mathscr{P}\varphi(I,\boldsymbol{x}),\mathscr{P}\varphi(I,\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime})
are also continuous functions, so the above equation converges to
0
whenever
|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}|\rightarrow 0
.
∎

Since
\mathbb{G}\times\Omega^{d-d_{r}}
is compact, the continuity of
\varphi:\mathbb{G}\times\Omega^{d-d_{r}}\rightarrow\mathbb{H},(R,\boldsymbol{x})\mapsto\varphi(R,\boldsymbol{x})

ensures that
\sup_{R,\boldsymbol{x}}\|\varphi(R,\boldsymbol{x})\|_{\mathbb{H}}
is finite, say
B>0
. Henceforth, we will use
B>0
instead of
B_{\boldsymbol{x}}
in (
S.2
).

Remark A.7
.

Any
SO(d)
-invariant Mercer kernel
K:\mathbb{B}^{d}\times\mathbb{B}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
on
\mathbb{B}^{d}
is a sinogram kernel associated to the
k
-plane transform in
Remark

A.2
. By the compactness of
\mathbb{B}^{d}
,
K
is bounded by some constant, say
(B/|\mathbb{B}^{d_{r}}|)^{2}>0
. Then, the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality yields

\displaystyle\sup_{\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathbb{B}^{d-d_{r}}}|\mathscr{P}_{I}f(\boldsymbol{x})|

\displaystyle=\left|\int_{(1-\|\boldsymbol{x}\|^{2})^{1/2}\cdot\mathbb{B}^{d_{r}}}f([\boldsymbol{x}:z])\mathrm{d}z\right|=\int_{(1-\|\boldsymbol{x}\|^{2})^{1/2}\cdot\mathbb{B}^{d_{r}}}|\langle f,k_{[\boldsymbol{x}:z]}\rangle_{\mathbb{H}}|\mathrm{d}z

\displaystyle\leq\int_{(1-\|\boldsymbol{x}\|^{2})^{1/2}\cdot\mathbb{B}^{d_{r}}}\|f\|_{\mathbb{H}}\frac{B}{|\mathbb{B}^{d_{r}}|}\mathrm{d}z\leq B\|f\|_{\mathbb{H}}.

We show that the range of the operator
\mathscr{P}
is again an RKHS.
Theorem

A.8
generalizes the central theorem in
[
117
]
with a simpler proof, and also incorporates the pull-back theorem (Theorem 5.7 in
[
79
]
).

Theorem A.8
.

Let
\mathscr{P}
be a sinogram operator and
K:\Omega^{d}\times\Omega^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be a sinogram kernel. Define bivariate functions
\tilde{K}^{I}:\Omega^{d-d_{r}}\times\Omega^{d-d_{r}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
and
\tilde{K}:(\mathbb{G}\times\Omega^{d-d_{r}})\times(\mathbb{G}\times\Omega^{d-d_{r}})\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
by

\displaystyle\tilde{K}^{I}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}):=\langle\varphi(I,\boldsymbol{x}),\varphi(I,\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime})\rangle_{\mathbb{H}},\quad\tilde{K}((R,\boldsymbol{x}),(R^{\prime},\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime})):=\langle\varphi(R,\boldsymbol{x}),\varphi(R^{\prime},\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime})\rangle_{\mathbb{H}}.

Then, both
\tilde{K}^{I}
and
\tilde{K}
are Mercer kernels. Also, if we restrict the domain of
\mathscr{P}_{R}
and
\mathscr{P}
to
\mathbb{H}(K)
, then both
\mathscr{P}_{R}:\mathbb{H}(K)\rightarrow\mathbb{H}(\tilde{K}^{I})
and
\mathscr{P}:\mathbb{H}(K)\rightarrow\mathbb{H}(\tilde{K})
are well-defined contractive, surjective operators. Considering the sinogram operator as an operator between RKHSs, the following holds for any
R\in\mathbb{G}
:

1.

\mathscr{P}_{R}^{*}:\mathbb{H}(\tilde{K}^{I})\rightarrow\mathbb{H}(K)
and
\mathscr{P}^{*}:\mathbb{H}(\tilde{K})\rightarrow\mathbb{H}(K)
are isometries, i.e.
\mathscr{P}_{R}\circ\mathscr{P}_{R}^{*}
are identity operators on
\mathbb{H}(\tilde{K}^{I})
and
\mathbb{H}(\tilde{K})
, respectively. Additionally, they share the same range given by

\mathcal{R}(\mathscr{P}^{*})=\mathcal{R}(\mathscr{P}_{R}^{*})=\overline{\spann\{\varphi(I,\boldsymbol{x}):\boldsymbol{x}\in\Omega^{d-d_{r}}\}}^{\mathbb{H}(K)},

where
\overline{\cdot}^{\mathbb{H}(K)}
denotes the closure in
\mathbb{H}(K)
.

2.

For
\tilde{K}^{I}
, it holds that
\tilde{k}^{I}_{\boldsymbol{x}}=\mathscr{P}_{R}\varphi(R,\boldsymbol{x})
and
\mathscr{P}_{R}^{*}\tilde{k}^{I}_{\boldsymbol{x}}=\varphi(R,\boldsymbol{x})
. For
\tilde{K}
, it holds that
\tilde{k}_{(R,\boldsymbol{x})}=\mathscr{P}\varphi(R,\boldsymbol{x})
and
\mathscr{P}^{*}\tilde{k}_{(R,\boldsymbol{x})}=\varphi(R,\boldsymbol{x})
.

3.

Both
\mathbb{H}(\tilde{K}^{I})
and
\mathbb{H}(\tilde{K})
are equipped with the range norm in the following sense
[
89
]
:

\displaystyle\|g_{R}\|_{\mathbb{H}(\tilde{K}^{I})}=\|\mathscr{P}_{R}^{*}g_{R}\|_{\mathbb{H}(K)}=\min\left\{\|f\|_{\mathbb{H}(K)}:g_{R}=\mathscr{P}_{R}f\right\},\quad g_{R}\in\mathbb{H}(\tilde{K}^{I}),

\displaystyle\|g\|_{\mathbb{H}(\tilde{K})}=\|\mathscr{P}^{*}g\|_{\mathbb{H}(K)}=\min\left\{\|f\|_{\mathbb{H}(K)}:g=\mathscr{P}f\right\},\quad g\in\mathbb{H}(\tilde{K}).

Proof.

First, note that for any
R\in\mathbb{G}
,
\tilde{K}^{I}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime})=\langle\varphi(R,\boldsymbol{x}),\varphi(R,\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime})\rangle_{\mathbb{H}}
from (
S.6
). It follows from
Proposition

A.6
that
\tilde{K}^{I}
is a Mercer kernel. Also, let
\mathbb{V}:=\spann\{\tilde{k}^{I}_{\boldsymbol{x}}:\boldsymbol{x}\in\Omega^{d-d_{r}}\}
, then
\mathbb{V}^{\perp}=\{0\}
, thus
\overline{\mathbb{V}}=\mathbb{H}(\tilde{K}^{I})
.
Now, for any
f\in\mathbb{H}(K)
,

\|f\|_{\mathbb{H}(K)}^{2}\tilde{K}^{I}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime})-\mathscr{P}_{R}f(\boldsymbol{x})\mathscr{P}_{R}f(\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime})=\|f\|_{\mathbb{H}(K)}^{2}\langle\varphi(R,\boldsymbol{x}),\varphi(R,\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime})\rangle_{\mathbb{H}}-\langle f,\varphi(R,\boldsymbol{x})\rangle_{\mathbb{H}}\langle f,\varphi(R,\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime})\rangle_{\mathbb{H}}

(S.7)

is a kernel function on
\Omega^{d-d_{r}}
due to Cauchy-Scwharz inequality. Then Theorem 3.11 in
[
79
]
reveals that
\mathscr{P}_{R}f\in\mathbb{H}(\tilde{K}^{I})
and
\|P_{R}f\|_{\mathbb{H}(\tilde{K}^{I})}\leq\|f\|_{\mathbb{H}(K)}
, i.e.
\mathscr{P}_{R}:\mathbb{H}(K)\rightarrow\mathbb{H}(\tilde{K}^{I})
is well-defined and is contractive.

For any
R\in\mathbb{G}
and
\boldsymbol{x}\in\Omega^{d-d_{r}}
, it holds from
Proposition

A.6
that

\displaystyle\tilde{k}^{I}_{\boldsymbol{x}}(\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime})=\tilde{K}^{I}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime})=\langle\varphi(R,\boldsymbol{x}),\varphi(R,\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime})\rangle_{\mathbb{H}(K)}=\mathscr{P}_{R}\varphi(R,\boldsymbol{x})(\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}),

\displaystyle\varphi(R,\boldsymbol{x})(\boldsymbol{z})=\mathscr{P}_{R}k_{\boldsymbol{z}}(\boldsymbol{x})=\langle\mathscr{P}_{R}k_{\boldsymbol{z}},\tilde{k}^{I}_{\boldsymbol{x}}\rangle_{\mathbb{H}(\tilde{K}^{I})}=\langle k_{\boldsymbol{z}},\mathscr{P}_{R}^{*}\tilde{k}^{I}_{\boldsymbol{x}}\rangle_{\mathbb{H}(\tilde{K})}=\mathscr{P}_{R}^{*}\tilde{k}^{I}_{\boldsymbol{x}}(\boldsymbol{z}),

i.e.,
\tilde{k}^{I}_{\boldsymbol{x}}=\mathscr{P}_{R}\varphi(R,\boldsymbol{x})
and
\mathscr{P}_{R}^{*}\tilde{k}^{I}_{\boldsymbol{x}}=\varphi(R,\boldsymbol{x})
.
Hence,
\mathscr{P}_{R}\mathscr{P}_{R}^{*}\tilde{k}^{I}_{\boldsymbol{x}}=\tilde{k}^{I}_{\boldsymbol{x}}
and because
\mathbb{V}
is dense in
\mathbb{H}(\tilde{K}^{I})
, we deduce that
\mathscr{P}_{R}\mathscr{P}_{R}^{*}
is the identity on
\mathbb{H}(\tilde{K}^{I})
, or equivalently,
\mathscr{P}_{R}^{*}
is a linear isometry.
As a consequence, we have that
\mathcal{R}(\mathscr{P}_{R})
is closed in
\mathbb{H}(\tilde{K}^{I})
, and
\mathcal{R}(\mathscr{P}_{R}^{*})
is closed in
\mathbb{H}(K)
due to Proposition 6.15 in
[
79
]
. Therefore,

\mathcal{R}(\mathscr{P}_{R})=\overline{\mathcal{R}(\mathscr{P}_{R})}\supset\overline{\mathbb{V}}=\mathbb{H}(\tilde{K}^{I}),

i.e.
\mathscr{P}_{R}
is surjective. Also,

\overline{\mathscr{P}_{R}^{*}\mathbb{V}}\subset\overline{\mathcal{R}(\mathscr{P}_{R}^{*})}=\mathcal{R}(\mathscr{P}_{R}^{*})=\mathscr{P}_{R}^{*}(\overline{\mathbb{V}})\subset\overline{\mathscr{P}_{R}^{*}\mathbb{V}},

hence
\mathscr{P}_{R}^{*}(\overline{\mathbb{V}})=\overline{\mathscr{P}_{R}^{*}\mathbb{V}}=\overline{\spann\{\varphi(R,\boldsymbol{x}):\boldsymbol{x}\in\Omega^{d-d_{r}}\}}
.

\mathcal{R}(\mathscr{P}_{R}^{*})=\overline{\mathscr{P}_{R}^{*}\mathbb{V}}=\overline{\spann\{\varphi(R,\boldsymbol{x}):\boldsymbol{x}\in\Omega^{d-d_{r}}\}}.

Now, for a sinogram operator
\mathscr{P}
, (
S.7
) and Theorem 3.11 in
[
79
]
shows again that
\mathscr{P}:\mathbb{H}(K)\rightarrow\mathbb{H}(\tilde{K})
is a well-defined contractive operator. Also, same as above using
Proposition

A.6
, we get
\tilde{k}_{(R,\boldsymbol{x})}=\mathscr{P}\varphi(R,\boldsymbol{x})
and
\mathscr{P}^{*}\tilde{k}_{(R,\boldsymbol{x})}=\varphi(R,\boldsymbol{x})
, and thus
\mathscr{P}^{*}
is a linear isometry. Continuing the same argument, we also conclude that
\mathscr{P}
is surjective, and
\mathcal{R}(\mathscr{P}^{*})=\overline{\spann\{\varphi(R,\boldsymbol{x}):\boldsymbol{x}\in\Omega^{d-d_{r}}\}}
.
∎

Note that the induced kernel
\tilde{K}
is also
\mathbb{G}
-invariant by (
S.6
): for any
R\in\mathbb{G}
, we have

\tilde{K}((R_{1},\boldsymbol{x}_{1}),(R_{2},\boldsymbol{x}_{2})):=\langle\varphi(R_{1},\boldsymbol{x}_{1}),\varphi(R_{2},\boldsymbol{x}_{2})\rangle_{\mathbb{H}}=\tilde{K}((RR_{1},\boldsymbol{x}_{1}),(RR_{2},\boldsymbol{x}_{2})).

(S.8)

Additionally,
\tilde{K},\tilde{K}^{I}
only depend on the sinogram operator
\mathscr{P}
and the original kernel
K
, not on the choice of the weight functions. For instance, when
\mathscr{P}
is the
k
-plane transform and
K(\boldsymbol{z}_{1},\boldsymbol{z}_{2})=\exp(-\gamma\|\boldsymbol{z}_{1}-\boldsymbol{z}_{2}\|^{2})
is a Gaussian kernel with the parameter
\gamma>0
, (
S.6
) yields that

\displaystyle\tilde{K}^{I}(\boldsymbol{x}_{1},\boldsymbol{x}_{2})=\exp(-\gamma\|\boldsymbol{x}_{1}-\boldsymbol{x}_{2}\|^{2})\times\int_{(1-\|\boldsymbol{x}\|^{2})^{1/2}\cdot\mathbb{B}^{d_{r}}}\int_{(1-\|\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}\|^{2})^{1/2}\cdot\mathbb{B}^{d_{r}}}\exp(-\gamma\|z_{1}-z_{2}\|^{2})\mathrm{d}z_{1}\mathrm{d}z_{2},

where the latter integral can be evaluated using the CDF of a multivariate normal distribution
[
117
]
.

Also, we remark that
\mathscr{P}\circ\imath
in
Section

6
is
not
equal to
\mathscr{P}:\mathbb{H}(K)\rightarrow\mathbb{H}(\tilde{K})
in
Theorem

A.8
as the codomains and the inner product structures differ. Indeed,
\mathscr{P}:\mathbb{H}(K)\rightarrow\mathbb{H}(\tilde{K})
is not even compact as its adjoint is isometric. To avoid confusion from now on,
\mathscr{P}
always implies the operator between
\mathcal{L}_{2}
spaces.

A.1
Direct Measurements

The standard FDA setting under direct measurements corresponds to the case where both
\mathbb{G}=\{I\}
and
\mathscr{P}_{I}=I
are trivial, thus every Mercer kernel on
\Omega^{d}
qualifies as a valid sinogram kernel. The feature map
\varphi(R,\boldsymbol{x})
reduces to the kernel generator
k_{\boldsymbol{x}}
, and the induced reproducing kernel
\tilde{K}
coincides with the original kernel. We briefly review Mercer’s theory here as a baseline for comparison in
Section

6
.

Under suitable boundary conditions, a common approach to constructing the kernel is via the Green’s function
G(\boldsymbol{z},\cdot)\in\mathcal{L}_{2}(\Omega^{d})
associated with a differential operator
\mathscr{D}
on
\mathcal{L}_{2}(\Omega^{d})
, which is injective and unbounded:

K(\boldsymbol{z}_{1},\boldsymbol{z}_{2})=\int_{\Omega^{d}}G(\boldsymbol{z}_{1},\boldsymbol{z}_{3})G(\boldsymbol{z}_{2},\boldsymbol{z}_{3})\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{z}_{3},\quad\boldsymbol{z}_{1},\boldsymbol{z}_{2}\in\Omega^{d}.

Fredholm theory
[
27
,
79
]
guarantees that the composition
\mathscr{D}\mathscr{T}_{G}
is the identity on
\mathcal{L}_{2}(\Omega^{d})
, yielding:

\mathbb{H}(K)=\text{Dom}(\mathscr{D}),\quad\|f\|_{\mathbb{H}(K)}=\|\mathscr{D}f\|_{\mathcal{L}_{2}(\Omega^{d})},

which provides an explicit interpretation of smoothness in terms of
\mathscr{D}
.
Furthermore, the s.p.d. compact operator
\mathscr{T}_{K}=\mathscr{T}_{G}\mathscr{T}_{G}^{*}
admits an eigen-decomposition with eigenvalue-eigenfunction pairs
(\tau_{l}^{2},f_{lm})
:

K(\boldsymbol{z}_{1},\boldsymbol{z}_{2})=\sum_{l=1}^{\infty}\tau_{l}^{2}\sum_{m=1}^{N(l)}f_{lm}(\boldsymbol{z}_{1})f_{lm}(\boldsymbol{z}_{2}),\quad\boldsymbol{z}_{1},\boldsymbol{z}_{2}\in\Omega^{d},

where
\tau_{1}>\tau_{2}>\dots>0
and
\{f_{lm}:l\in\mathbb{N},1\leq m\leq N(l)\}
forms a CONS in
\mathcal{L}_{2}(\Omega^{d})
.

Example A.9
.

Let
\mathscr{D}=\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}z}
on
\Omega^{d}=[0,1]
with the boundary condition
f(0)=0
. Then the Green’s function yields:

K(z_{1},z_{2})=\int_{0}^{1}I(z_{3}<z_{1})I(z_{3}<z_{2})\mathrm{d}z_{3}=\min(z_{1},z_{2}),\quad 0\leq z_{1},z_{2}\leq 1.

The Mercer decomposition gives
N(l)=1
,
\tau_{l}=[(l-1/2)\pi]^{-1}
, and
f_{l}(z)=\sqrt{2}\sin((l-1/2)\pi z)
for each
l\in\mathbb{N}
. More generally, let
p\in\mathbb{N}
, and
\mathscr{D}=\frac{\mathrm{d}^{p}}{\mathrm{d}z^{p}}
with boundary conditions
f(0)=f^{\prime}(0)=\cdots=f^{(p-1)}(0)=0
. Then, integration by parts leads to
\mathscr{D}^{*}=(-1)^{p}\frac{\mathrm{d}^{p}}{\mathrm{d}z^{p}}
with boundary conditions
f(1)=f^{\prime}(1)=\cdots=f^{(p-1)}(1)=0
, and
f_{l}
’s are the eigenfunctions of
\mathscr{D}^{*}\mathscr{D}=(-1)^{p}\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2p}}{\mathrm{d}z^{2p}}
with corresponding boundary conditions
[
15
]
. Consequently, the kernel for the Sobolev space of order
p
becomes

\displaystyle K(z_{1},z_{2})=\frac{B_{p}(z_{1})B_{p}(z_{2})}{p!}-\frac{B_{2p}(|z_{1}-z_{2}|)}{(2p)!},\quad 0\leq z_{1},z_{2}\leq 1,

where
B_{p}
is the Bernoulli polynomial of degree
p
, which is the setup considered in
[
86
,
6
]
.

If the operator
\mathscr{D}
and its boundary conditions are invariant under a group action, then the resulting kernel inherits this symmetry:

Example A.10
.

[
100
,
9
]
consider the spherical Sobolev space of order
p>d/2
, arising from the pseudo-differential operator
\mathscr{D}=(I-\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}})^{p/2}
. Note that
\Omega_{d}=\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
has no boundary and the Laplace-Beltrami operator
\mathop{}\!\mathbin{\bigtriangleup}_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}
is isotropic.
In this case, the eigenfunctions
f_{lm}=Y_{lm}
are the spherical harmonics of degree
l
, with corresponding eigenvalues and multiplicities:

\tau_{l}=[1+l(l+d-2)]^{-p/2}\asymp l^{-p},\quad N(l)=\begin{pmatrix}l+d-1\\
l\end{pmatrix}-\begin{pmatrix}l+d-3\\
l-2\end{pmatrix}\asymp l^{d-1}.

(S.9)

Thus, the reproducing kernel becomes isotropic and admits the expansion:

\displaystyle K(\theta_{1},\theta_{2})=\frac{1}{|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|}\sum_{l=1}^{\infty}\tau_{l}^{2}N(l)C_{l}^{d/2}(\theta_{1}^{\top}\theta_{2}),\quad\theta_{1},\theta_{2}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1},

where
C_{l}^{d/2-1}
is the Gegenbauer polynomial of degree
l
, normalized such that
C_{l}^{d/2}(1)=1

[
17
]
.

However, when
\partial\Omega^{d}\neq\emptyset
, the boundary conditions can break symmetries. For instance, in
Example

A.9
, although
\mathscr{D}
is translation-invariant, its domain
[0,1]
and boundary conditions are not. Consequently, the resulting reproducing kernel does not inherit translation invariance. If we consider
\mathbb{G}=SO(d)
instead of
\mathbb{G}=\{I\}
in
Example

A.10
, we get the following interpretation:

Remark A.11
.

Consider again the setting in
Example

A.10
, where
\Omega^{d}=\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
,
\mathbb{G}=SO(d)
, and
\mathscr{P}_{I}=I
so that
\mathscr{P}=\rho
. In this case, the eigenvalues
\tau_{l}^{2}
and multiplicities
N(l)
are as given in (
S.9
), and the eigenfunctions are
e_{lm}=\tau_{l}Y_{lm}
, where
Y_{lm}
are the spherical harmonics of degree
l
. The irreducible sub-representation
[\rho(R)]_{\{e_{lm}:1\leq m\leq N(l)\}}\in SO(N(l))
corresponds precisely to the Wigner D-matrix
[
115
,
17
]
, and

g_{lm}(R,\theta)=\rho(R)Y_{lm}(\theta)=Y_{lm}(R^{-1}\theta).

Since
\tilde{K}_{l}
in (
16
) is isotropic and
SO(d)
acts transitively on
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
, it follows that
\tilde{K}_{l}((R_{1},\theta_{1}),(R_{2},\theta_{2}))
depends only on the inner product between
R_{1}^{-1}\theta_{1},R_{2}^{-1}\theta_{2}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
. As a result,
Proposition

6.3
implies

\tilde{K}_{l}((R,\theta),(R,\theta))\equiv\frac{N(l)}{|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|},\quad l\in\mathbb{N},\,R\in SO(d),\,\theta\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}.

In general, the
l
-th kernel takes the form of the Funk-Hecke formula
[
17
]
:

\displaystyle\tilde{K}_{l}((R_{1},\theta_{1}),(R_{2},\theta_{2}))=\frac{N(l)}{|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|}C_{l}^{d/2-1}(\theta_{1}^{\top}R_{1}R_{2}^{\top}\theta_{2}),\quad R_{1},R_{2}\in SO(d),\,\theta_{1},\theta_{2}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}.

This recovers the original kernel as
K(\theta_{1},\theta_{2})=\tilde{K}((I,\theta_{1}),(I,\theta_{2}))=\sum_{l=1}^{\infty}\tau_{l}^{2}\tilde{K}_{l}((I,\theta_{1}),(I,\theta_{2}))
.

B
Implementation

This section, heavily relies on our recent work
[
118
]
, outlines the practical implementation of our mean and covariance estimators, guided by the representer theorem in
Theorem

5.4
. For simplicity, we will assume a uniform number of tilts (
r_{i}\equiv r
) and locations (
s_{ij}\equiv s
) per particle, though the methods extend to non-uniform cases. Our code and column-major indexing conventions follow the order of
i
(particle) →
j
(tilt) →
k
(location). Throughout, block partitioning is performed with respect to the index
i=1,\cdots,n
, corresponding to each random function.

B.1
Mean Estimation

Modify the vectorization of observations and the mean Gram matrix introduced in
Section

5.2
as:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{w}:=\mathrm{vec}\left[Z_{ijk}\right]=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\boldsymbol{w}_{1}\\
\hline\cr\boldsymbol{w}_{2}\\
\hline\cr\vdots\\
\hline\cr\boldsymbol{w}_{n}\end{array}\right)\in\mathbb{R}^{nrs},\,\boldsymbol{\Phi}:=\left[\langle\varphi_{i_{1}j_{1}k_{1}},\varphi_{i_{2}j_{2}k_{2}}\rangle_{\mathbb{H}}\right]=\left(\begin{array}[]{c|c|c|c}\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{11}&\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{12}&\cdots&\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{1n}\\
\hline\cr\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{21}&\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{22}&\cdots&\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{2n}\\
\hline\cr\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
\hline\cr\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{n1}&\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{n2}&\cdots&\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{nn}\end{array}\right)\in\mathbb{R}^{nrs\times nrs}.

We then consider the
rescaled
empirical risk functional
\hat{R}_{\nu}:\mathbb{H}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
with tuning parameter
\nu>0
:

\displaystyle\hat{R}_{\nu}(f)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{r}\sum_{k=1}^{s}\left(Z_{ijk}-\langle f,\varphi_{ijk}\rangle_{\mathbb{H}}\right)^{2}+\nu\|f\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}.

Since
\hat{R}_{\nu\cdot nrs/\omega_{d_{r},d}}
is proportional to
\hat{L}_{\nu}
defined in (
8
), no essential change is made but it leads to a cleaner normal equation:

\displaystyle\hat{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{\nu}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{r}\sum_{k=1}^{s}\hat{\alpha}^{\nu}_{ijk}\varphi_{ijk},\quad\hat{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{\nu}:=\mathrm{vec}[\hat{\alpha}^{\nu}_{ijk}]=\left(\boldsymbol{\Phi}+\nu\boldsymbol{I}\right)^{-1}\boldsymbol{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{nrs}.

If
\boldsymbol{R}_{ij}\in\mathbb{G}
exhibits a parallel imaging geometry,
\boldsymbol{\Phi}
has a
n\times n
block-circulant structure, allowing for even faster solves using FFTs, see Section 5 in
[
117
]
.
To visualize the result, we can evaluate
\hat{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{\nu}\in\mathbb{H}
on a grid of points
\mathcal{G}:=\{\boldsymbol{z}_{t}:1\leq t\leq T\}\subset\Omega^{d}
. This is done by first computing a
frame
matrix:

\boldsymbol{F}:=[F_{t,ijk}:=\varphi_{ijk}(\boldsymbol{z}_{t})]\in\mathbb{R}^{T\times nrs}

with entries computed using (
S.4
). Then, the evaluation of the mean estimator over the grid is simply:

\hat{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{\nu}:=[\hat{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{\nu}(\boldsymbol{z}_{1}),\hat{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{\nu}(\boldsymbol{z}_{2}),\dots,\hat{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{\nu}(\boldsymbol{z}_{T})]^{\top}=\boldsymbol{F}\hat{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{\nu}\in\mathbb{R}^{T}.

B.2
Covariance Estimation

While the mean estimation is straightforward, a naïve approach to the covariance estimation could result in a severe computational burden. The normal equation for the covariance coefficients involves the massive covariance Gram matrix
\boldsymbol{\Phi}^{\odot}
. Although one may attempt to solve this large linear system via Cholesky or spectral decomposition of the positive definite matrix
\boldsymbol{\Phi}^{\odot}+\eta\boldsymbol{I}
, this approach is impractical: due to the involvement of the Khatri-Rao product
[
52
,
71
]
, the decompositions of
\boldsymbol{\Phi}
do not translate into those of
\boldsymbol{\Phi}^{\odot}
.
Therefore, our solution is to never form the
\boldsymbol{\Phi}^{\odot}
matrix in solving
Theorem

5.4
. Instead, we use the
TReK
algorithm
[
118
]
– a tensorized variant of the Krylov-projection method under range restrictions – to iteratively solve
Theorem

5.4
, as we now briefly describe. These
element-free
solvers only require a function that computes the matrix-vector product, but the tensor-matrix product in our case, which we now briefly describe.

To formalize the element-free computation, we first define
\odiag(\mathbb{R}^{n(rs)}\otimes\mathbb{R}^{n(rs)})
as the space of
n\times n
diagonal block matrices, where each block is an
\mathbb{R}^{(rs)\times(rs)}
matrix:

\boldsymbol{A}=\odiag[\boldsymbol{A}_{i}]=\left(\begin{array}[]{c|c|c|c}\boldsymbol{A}_{1}&\boldsymbol{0}&\dots&\boldsymbol{0}\\
\hline\cr\boldsymbol{0}&\boldsymbol{A}_{2}&\dots&\boldsymbol{0}\\
\hline\cr\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
\hline\cr\boldsymbol{0}&\boldsymbol{0}&\dots&\boldsymbol{A}_{n}\end{array}\right)\in\odiag(\mathbb{R}^{n(rs)}\otimes\mathbb{R}^{n(rs)})\subset\mathbb{R}^{n(rs)\times n(rs)}.

The block outer product of the observation vector is then
\boldsymbol{w}\odot\boldsymbol{w}:=\odiag[\boldsymbol{w}_{i}\boldsymbol{w}_{i}^{\top}]\in\odiag(\mathbb{R}^{n(rs)}\otimes\mathbb{R}^{n(rs)})
. We also define a block outer product for the Gram matrix
\boldsymbol{\Phi}\in\mathbb{R}^{n(rs)\times n(rs)}
that maps a diagonal block matrix to another:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Phi}\odot\boldsymbol{\Phi}:

\displaystyle\odiag[\boldsymbol{A}_{i}]_{i=1}^{n}\in\odiag(\mathbb{R}^{n(rs)}\otimes\mathbb{R}^{n(rs)})\mapsto

\displaystyle\hskip 30.00005pt\odiag\left[\sum_{i^{\prime}=1}^{n}\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{ii^{\prime}}\boldsymbol{A}_{i^{\prime}}\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{i^{\prime}i}\right]_{i=1}^{n}\in\odiag(\mathbb{R}^{n(rs)}\otimes\mathbb{R}^{n(rs)}).

The pseudocode for this operation is presented in
Algorithm

1
.

Algorithm 1

Block Outer Product

1:

\boldsymbol{\Phi}\in\mathbb{R}^{\boldsymbol{r}\times\boldsymbol{r}},\boldsymbol{A}=\odiag[\boldsymbol{A}_{i}]\in\odiag(\mathbb{R}^{n(rs)}\otimes\mathbb{R}^{n(rs)})

2:

\boldsymbol{B}=(\boldsymbol{\Phi}\odot\boldsymbol{\Phi})\boldsymbol{A}\in\odiag(\mathbb{R}^{n(rs)}\otimes\mathbb{R}^{n(rs)})

3:

for

i=1
to
n

do

4:

\boldsymbol{B}_{i}\leftarrow\boldsymbol{0}

5:

for

i^{\prime}=1
to
n

do

6:

\boldsymbol{B}_{i}\leftarrow\boldsymbol{B}_{i}+\boldsymbol{K}_{ii^{\prime}}\boldsymbol{A}_{i^{\prime}}\boldsymbol{K}_{ii^{\prime}}^{\top}

Moving forward, we introduce the index set adhering to our off-diagonal pair observation scheme:

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{i}:=\{(J_{1},J_{2})=(j_{1},k_{1},j_{2},k_{2}):1\leq j_{1}\neq j_{2}\leq r,1\leq k_{1},k_{2}\leq s\},\quad|\mathcal{J}_{i}|=r(r-1)s^{2}.

(S.10)

Then, we define the
i
-th elimination operator for each
i
-th block with respect to
\mathcal{J}_{i}
:

\mathscr{J}_{i}:\mathbb{R}^{(rs)\times(rs)}\to\mathbb{R}^{(rs)\times(rs)},\quad(\mathscr{J}_{i}\boldsymbol{A}_{i})[J_{1},J_{2}]=\begin{cases}\boldsymbol{A}_{i}[J_{1},J_{2}]&,\quad(J_{1},J_{2})\in\mathcal{J}_{i}\\
0&,\quad(J_{1},J_{2})\notin\mathcal{J}_{i}\end{cases},

zeroes out the diagonal blocks of
\boldsymbol{A}_{i}
. Accordingly, the elimination operator acts block-wise:

\mathscr{J}=\odiag[\mathscr{J}_{i}]:\odiag[\boldsymbol{A}_{i}]\in\odiag(\mathbb{R}^{n(rs)}\otimes\mathbb{R}^{n(rs)})\mapsto\odiag[\mathscr{J}_{i}\boldsymbol{A}_{i}]\in\odiag(\mathbb{R}^{n(rs)}\otimes\mathbb{R}^{n(rs)}),

This is an orthogonal projection, with pseudocode provided in
Algorithm

2
.

Algorithm 2

Elimination of Irrelevant Pairs

1:

\boldsymbol{A}=\odiag[\boldsymbol{A}_{i}]\in\odiag(\mathbb{R}^{n(rs)}\otimes\mathbb{R}^{n(rs)})

2:

\boldsymbol{B}=\mathscr{J}\boldsymbol{A}\in\odiag(\mathbb{R}^{n(rs)}\otimes\mathbb{R}^{n(rs)})

3:

\boldsymbol{B}\leftarrow\boldsymbol{A}

4:

for

i=1
to
n

do

5:

for

j=1
to
r

do

6:

for

k,k^{\prime}=1
to
s

do

7:

\boldsymbol{B}_{i}[(j,k),(j,k^{\prime})]\leftarrow 0

The
rescaled
empirical risk functional
\hat{R}_{\eta}^{\odot}:\mathbb{H}\otimes\mathbb{H}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
for the second moment with tuning parameter
\eta>0
is:

\displaystyle\hat{R}_{\eta}^{\odot}(G)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{(J_{1},J_{2})\in\mathcal{J}}\left(Z_{iJ_{1}J_{2}}-\left\langle G,\varphi_{iJ_{1}J_{2}}^{\otimes}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{H}\otimes\mathbb{H}}\right)^{2}+\eta\|G\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}\otimes\mathbb{H}}.

Note that
\hat{R}_{(\eta n|\mathcal{J}|/\omega_{d_{r},d}^{2})}^{\odot}(G)\propto\hat{L}_{\eta}^{\odot}(G)
in (
5.2
) whenever
G\in\mathbb{H}\otimes\mathbb{H}
is a self-adjoint operator due to the symmetry:
Z_{iJ_{1}J_{2}}=Z_{iJ_{2}J_{1}}
and
(\varphi_{iJ_{1}J_{2}}^{\otimes})^{*}=\varphi_{iJ_{2}J_{1}}^{\otimes}
. The unique minimizer of
\hat{R}_{\eta}^{\odot}
is a linear combination of symmetrized tensor products of feature maps:

\displaystyle\hat{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}_{\eta}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{(J_{1},J_{2})\in\mathcal{J}}\hat{\alpha}^{\odot}_{iJ_{1}J_{2}}\frac{\varphi_{ij_{1}k_{1}}\otimes\varphi_{ij_{2}k_{2}}+\varphi_{ij_{2}k_{2}}\otimes\varphi_{ij_{1}k_{1}}}{2},

If we arrange its coefficients into a block-diagonal matrix that lies in the range of
\mathscr{J}
:

\displaystyle\hat{\boldsymbol{A}}_{\eta}:=\odiag[(\hat{\boldsymbol{A}}_{\eta})_{i}]\in\mathcal{R}(\mathscr{J})\subset\odiag(\mathbb{R}^{n(rs)}\otimes\mathbb{R}^{n(rs)}),

(S.11)

then this coefficient matrix is the unique solution to the normal equation:

[\mathscr{J}(\boldsymbol{\Phi}\odot\boldsymbol{\Phi})\mathscr{J}+\lambda\mathscr{I}]\hat{\boldsymbol{A}}_{\eta}=\mathscr{J}(\boldsymbol{w}\odot\boldsymbol{w}).

(S.12)

where
\mathscr{I}
is the identity operator on
\odiag(\mathbb{R}^{n(rs)}\otimes\mathbb{R}^{n(rs)})
, see Theorem 3.2 in
[
118
]
.
The action of the large positive definite operator on the left hand side can be implemented using
\mathscr{J}(\boldsymbol{\Phi}\odot\boldsymbol{\Phi})\mathscr{J}
using
Algorithms

1
and
2
, allowing the system to be solved with an iterative Krylov method like CG or LSQR
[
87
,
40
,
75
]
.
Once (
S.12
) is solved, the bivariate function
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}_{\eta}:\Omega^{d}\times\Omega^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
can be visualized over the square grid
\mathcal{G}\times\mathcal{G}
via the following quadratic form:

[\hat{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}_{\eta}(\boldsymbol{z}_{t_{1}},\boldsymbol{z}_{t_{2}})]_{1\leq t_{1},t_{2}\leq T}=\boldsymbol{F}\hat{\boldsymbol{A}}_{\eta}\boldsymbol{F}^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{T\times T}.

Accordingly, the covariance estimator is evaluated as
\boldsymbol{F}(\hat{\boldsymbol{A}}_{\eta}-\hat{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{\nu}\hat{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{\nu}^{\top})\boldsymbol{F}^{\top}
.

Remark B.1
.

The constraint structure (
S.11
) reveals that
\text{tr}(\hat{\boldsymbol{A}}_{\eta})=0
, hence
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}_{\eta}
fails to be s.p.d. unless it is zero. This issue arises fundamentally from using only off-diagonal pairs and is not an artifact of the inverse problem itself. Notably, the same phenomenon persists even when directly estimating the covariance function from centered observations. This underscores the necessity of the subsequent functional PCA step to obtain a s.p.d. covariance estimate by truncating the negative spectrum.

The final step is to perform an eigendecomposition of the estimated covariance operator. The following theorem connects the kernel PCA to a generalized eigenvalue problem, see
[
118
]
for the proof.

Theorem B.2
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\Phi}\in\mathbb{R}^{n(rs)\times n(rs)}
be the mean Gram matrix, and consider the covariance tensor of the form

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Sigma}=\sum_{i_{1},i_{2}=1}^{n}\sum_{j_{1},j_{2}=1}^{r}\sum_{k_{1},k_{2}=1}^{s}\boldsymbol{C}_{i_{1}j_{1}k_{1},i_{2}j_{2}k_{2}}\,(\varphi_{i_{1}j_{1}k_{1}}\otimes\varphi_{i_{2}j_{2}k_{2}}),

where
\boldsymbol{C}\in\mathbb{R}^{n(rs)\times n(rs)}
is symmetric. Let
q=\text{rank}(\boldsymbol{C}\boldsymbol{\Phi})
,
(\lambda_{k},\psi_{l}=\sum_{l=1}^{p}v_{ijk,l}\varphi_{ijk})
denote the
l
-th eigenpair of
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
, i.e.
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}=\sum_{l=1}^{q}\lambda_{l}\psi_{l}\otimes\psi_{l}
. If we define

\boldsymbol{\Lambda}=\odiag[\lambda_{l}]_{1\leq l\leq q}\in\mathbb{R}^{q\times q},\quad\boldsymbol{V}=[v_{ijk,l}]=[\boldsymbol{v}_{1}|\cdots|\boldsymbol{v}_{q}]\in\mathbb{R}^{n(rs)\times q},

then the diagonal matrix
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}
and
\boldsymbol{G}
-orthogonal matrix
\boldsymbol{V}
satisfy the generalized eigenvalue problem:

\boldsymbol{C}\boldsymbol{\Phi}\boldsymbol{V}=\boldsymbol{V}\boldsymbol{\Lambda},\quad\boldsymbol{V}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Phi}\boldsymbol{V}=\boldsymbol{I}

Also, when
\boldsymbol{\Phi}
is rank-deficient, the kernel PCA does not depend on the choice of
\boldsymbol{V}
.

Applying
Theorem

B.2
with
\boldsymbol{C}=\hat{\boldsymbol{A}}_{\eta}-\hat{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{\nu}\hat{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{\nu}^{\top}
yields the spectral decomposition of the covariance estimator
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{\nu,\eta}\in\mathbb{H}\otimes\mathbb{H}
. If a s.p.d. estimator is desired, the components corresponding to negative eigenvalues can be truncated. If we are only interested in the dominant principal components, incomplete factorization can be done using a version of Lanczos tridiagonalization
[
56
,
74
]
, see
[
118
]
and references therein.

C
Proofs

C.1
Proofs in Section
5

Proof of
Proposition

5.2
.

1.

For all
1\leq i\leq n,1\leq j\leq r_{i},1\leq k\leq s_{ij}
, it is straightforward that
0=\mathscr{P}_{\boldsymbol{R}_{ij}}f(\boldsymbol{X}_{ijk})=\langle f,\varphi_{ijk}\rangle_{\mathbb{H}}
if and only if
f\in(\mathbb{H}_{\boldsymbol{F}})^{\perp}
.

2.

For all
1\leq i\leq n,1\leq j\neq j^{\prime}\leq r_{i},1\leq k\leq s_{ij},1\leq k^{\prime}\leq s_{ij^{\prime}}
,

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=(\mathscr{P}_{\boldsymbol{R}_{ij}}\otimes\mathscr{P}_{\boldsymbol{R}_{ij^{\prime}}})G(\boldsymbol{X}_{ijk},\boldsymbol{X}_{ij^{\prime}k^{\prime}})=\langle G,\varphi_{ijk}\otimes\varphi_{ij^{\prime}k^{\prime}}\rangle_{\mathbb{H}\otimes\mathbb{H}},

if and only if
G\in((\mathbb{H}\otimes\mathbb{H})_{\boldsymbol{F}})^{\perp}
.

∎

Proof of
Proposition

5.3
.

Given
f\in\mathbb{H}
, decompose it by
f=f_{\boldsymbol{F}}\oplus(f-f_{\boldsymbol{F}})\in\mathbb{H}_{\boldsymbol{F}}\oplus(\mathbb{H}_{\boldsymbol{F}})^{\perp}
. Then by
Proposition

5.2
, we get

\hat{L}_{\nu}(f)=\hat{L}_{\nu}(f_{\boldsymbol{F}})+\nu\|f-f_{\boldsymbol{F}}\|^{2}\geq\hat{L}_{\nu}(f_{\boldsymbol{F}}),

thus any minimizer of
\hat{L}_{\nu}
should belong to
\mathbb{H}_{\boldsymbol{F}}\subset\mathbb{H}
. Moreover, the minimizer of
\hat{L}_{\nu}
is unique since
\hat{L}_{\nu}
is strictly convex in
f\in\mathbb{H}
, provided that
\nu>0
. Applying the same rationale, one can show that the minimizer of
\hat{L}_{\eta}^{\odot}
is unique, and it belongs to
(\mathbb{H}\otimes\mathbb{H})_{\boldsymbol{F}}
. Finally, since
\hat{L}_{\eta}^{\odot}(G)=\hat{L}_{\eta}^{\odot}(G^{*})
,
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}_{\eta}
is self-adjoint due to its uniqueness.
∎

Proof of
Theorem

5.4
.

Since

\displaystyle\mathrm{vec}\left[\frac{\langle\hat{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{\nu},\varphi_{ijk}\rangle_{\mathbb{H}}}{\sqrt{r_{i}s_{ij}}}\right]=\mathrm{vec}\left[\frac{1}{\sqrt{r_{i}s_{ij}}}\langle\sum_{i^{\prime}=1}^{n}\sum_{j^{\prime}=1}^{r_{i}}\sum_{k^{\prime}=1}^{s_{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{r_{i^{\prime}}s_{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}}}}\hat{\alpha}^{\nu}_{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}k^{\prime}}\varphi_{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}k^{\prime}},\varphi_{ijk}\rangle_{\mathbb{H}}\right]=\boldsymbol{\Phi}\hat{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{\nu},

\displaystyle\|\hat{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{\nu}\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}=\left\|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{r_{i}}\sum_{k=1}^{s_{ij}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{r_{i}s_{ij}}}\hat{\alpha}^{\nu}_{ijk}\varphi_{ijk}\right\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}=\hat{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{\nu}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Phi}\hat{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{\nu},

we get

\displaystyle\hat{L}_{\nu}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{\nu})

\displaystyle:=\frac{\omega_{d_{r},d}}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{r_{i}}\sum_{k=1}^{s_{ij}}\left(\frac{Z_{ijk}}{\sqrt{r_{i}s_{ij}}}-\frac{\langle\hat{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{\nu},\varphi_{ijk}\rangle_{\mathbb{H}}}{\sqrt{r_{i}s_{ij}}}\right)^{2}+\nu\|\hat{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{\nu}\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\omega_{d_{r},d}}{n}\|\boldsymbol{w}-\boldsymbol{\Phi}\hat{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{\nu}\|^{2}+\nu\hat{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{\nu}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Phi}\hat{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{\nu}.

This yields the normal equation as follows:

\displaystyle\left(\boldsymbol{\Phi}^{2}+\frac{n\nu}{\omega_{d_{r},d}}\boldsymbol{\Phi}\right)\hat{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{\nu}=\boldsymbol{\Phi}\boldsymbol{w}\,

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow\,\boldsymbol{\Phi}\hat{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{\nu}=\boldsymbol{\Phi}\left(\boldsymbol{\Phi}+\frac{n\nu}{\omega_{d_{r},d}}\boldsymbol{I}\right)^{-1}\boldsymbol{w}.

When
\boldsymbol{\Phi}
is not strictly positive, then the normal equation does not have a unique solution, but nonetheless they all yield the same
\hat{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{\nu}
by its uniqueness from
Proposition

5.3
. Thus, we might take
\hat{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{\nu}=(\boldsymbol{\Phi}+\omega_{d_{r},d}^{-1}n\nu\boldsymbol{I})^{-1}\boldsymbol{w}
.

For the covariance, note that
\langle\varphi_{i_{1}j_{1}k_{1}j^{\prime}_{1}k^{\prime}_{1}}^{\otimes},\varphi_{i_{2}j_{2}k_{2}j^{\prime}_{2}k^{\prime}_{2}}^{\otimes}\rangle_{\mathbb{H}\otimes\mathbb{H}}=\langle\varphi_{i_{1}j_{1}k_{1}},\varphi_{i_{2}j_{2}k_{2}}\rangle_{\mathbb{H}}\langle\varphi_{i_{1}j^{\prime}_{1}k^{\prime}_{1}},\varphi_{i_{2}j^{\prime}_{2}k^{\prime}_{2}}\rangle_{\mathbb{H}}
. Similar to above, one can show that

\displaystyle\hat{L}_{\eta}^{\odot}(G)

\displaystyle=\frac{\omega_{d_{r},d}^{2}}{n}\|\boldsymbol{w}^{\odot}-\boldsymbol{\Phi}^{\odot}\hat{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{\eta}^{\odot}\|^{2}+\eta(\hat{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{\eta}^{\odot})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Phi}\hat{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{\nu}^{\odot},

thus

\hat{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{\eta}^{\odot}=\left(\boldsymbol{\Phi}^{\odot}+\frac{n\eta}{\omega_{d_{r},d}^{2}}\boldsymbol{I}\right)^{-1}\boldsymbol{w}^{\odot}.

∎

C.2
Proofs in Section
6

Proof of
Proposition

6.1
.

Let
g\in\mathcal{L}_{2}(\mathbb{G}\times\Omega^{d-d_{r}},W_{d_{r},d})
be given. Since
\tilde{k}_{(R_{1},\boldsymbol{x}_{1})}=\mathscr{P}\varphi(R_{1},\boldsymbol{x}_{1})
from
Theorem

A.8
, we have

\displaystyle\mathscr{T}_{\tilde{K}}g(R_{1},\boldsymbol{x}_{1})

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{G}}\int_{\Omega^{d-d_{r}}}\tilde{K}((R_{1},\boldsymbol{x}_{1}),(R_{2},\boldsymbol{x}_{2}))g(R_{2},\boldsymbol{x}_{2})W_{d_{r},d}(\boldsymbol{x}_{2})\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}_{2}\mathrm{d}R_{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{G}}\int_{\Omega^{d-d_{r}}}[\mathscr{P}\varphi(R_{1},\boldsymbol{x}_{1})](R_{2},\boldsymbol{x}_{2})g(R_{2},\boldsymbol{x}_{2})W_{d_{r},d}(\boldsymbol{x}_{2})\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}_{2}\mathrm{d}R_{2}

\displaystyle=\langle\mathscr{P}\varphi(R_{1},\boldsymbol{x}_{1}),g\rangle_{\mathcal{L}_{2}(\mathbb{G}\times\Omega^{d-d_{r}},W_{d_{r},d})}=\langle\varphi(R_{1},\boldsymbol{x}_{1}),\mathscr{P}^{*}g\rangle_{\mathcal{L}_{2}(\Omega^{d},W_{0,d})}

\displaystyle=\langle\varphi(R_{1},\boldsymbol{x}_{1}),\mathscr{T}_{K}\mathscr{P}^{*}g\rangle_{\mathbb{H}}=[\mathscr{P}\mathscr{T}_{K}\mathscr{P}^{*}g](R_{1},\boldsymbol{x}_{1}),

by the reproducing property. Therefore,
\mathscr{T}_{\tilde{K}}=\mathscr{P}\mathscr{T}_{K}\mathscr{P}^{*}
.
∎

Proof of
Proposition

6.2
.

To show the uniqueness of
\mathscr{K}
, it suffices to show (
15
). For any
f_{1},f_{2}\in\mathbb{H}
, by Theorem 7.2.5 in
[
42
]
,

\displaystyle\omega_{d_{r},d}\langle\mathbb{E}[\varphi(\boldsymbol{R},\boldsymbol{X})\otimes\varphi(\boldsymbol{R},\boldsymbol{X})]f_{1},f_{2}\rangle_{\mathbb{H}}

\displaystyle=\omega_{d_{r},d}\mathbb{E}[\langle\varphi(\boldsymbol{R},\boldsymbol{X}),f_{1}\rangle_{\mathbb{H}}\langle\varphi(\boldsymbol{R},\boldsymbol{X}),f_{2}\rangle_{\mathbb{H}}]

\displaystyle=\omega_{d_{r},d}\mathbb{E}[\mathscr{P}_{\boldsymbol{R}}f_{1}(\boldsymbol{X})\mathscr{P}_{\boldsymbol{R}}f_{2}(\boldsymbol{X})]

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{G}}\int_{\Omega^{d-d_{r}}}\mathscr{P}_{R}f_{1}(\boldsymbol{x})\mathscr{P}_{R}f_{2}(\boldsymbol{x})W_{d_{r},d}(\boldsymbol{x})\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\mathrm{d}R

\displaystyle=\langle\mathscr{P}f_{1},\mathscr{P}f_{2}\rangle_{\mathcal{L}_{2}(\Omega^{d-d_{r}},W_{d_{r},d})}.

Also,
{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\mathscr{K}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{1}=\mathbb{E}[\|\varphi(\boldsymbol{R},\boldsymbol{X})\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{2}]\leq B^{2}
by Theorem 7.2.5 in
[
42
]
, thus
\mathscr{K}
is of trace-class.
∎

Proof of
Proposition

6.3
.

For each
l\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{G}}\int_{\Omega^{d-d_{r}}}\tilde{K}_{l}((R_{1},\boldsymbol{x}_{1}),(R_{3},\boldsymbol{x}_{3}))\tilde{K}_{l}((R_{2},\boldsymbol{x}_{2}),(R_{3},\boldsymbol{x}_{3}))W_{d_{r},d}(\boldsymbol{x})\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\mathrm{d}R

\displaystyle=\sum_{m=1}^{N(l)}g_{lm}(R_{1},\boldsymbol{x}_{1})\sum_{m^{\prime}=1}^{N(l)}g_{lm^{\prime}}(R_{2},\boldsymbol{x}_{2})\langle g_{lm},\mathscr{P}g_{lm^{\prime}}\rangle_{W_{d_{r},d}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{m=1}^{N(l)}g_{lm}(R_{1},\boldsymbol{x}_{1})g_{lm}(R_{2},\boldsymbol{x}_{2})=\tilde{K}_{l}((R_{1},\boldsymbol{x}_{1}),(R_{2},\boldsymbol{x}_{2})).

For any
g\in\mathcal{L}_{2}(\mathbb{G}\times\Omega^{d-d_{r}},W_{d_{r},d})
, decompose it by

g=\sum_{l=1}^{\infty}g_{l}+g_{\perp}\in\bigoplus_{l=1}^{\infty}\tilde{\mathcal{H}}_{l}\oplus\mathcal{R}(\mathscr{P}\circ\imath)^{\perp},

where
g_{l}\in\tilde{\mathcal{H}}_{l}
and
g_{\perp}\in\mathcal{R}(\mathscr{P}\circ\imath)^{\perp}
. Then

\displaystyle\mathscr{T}_{\tilde{K}_{l}}g(R_{1},\boldsymbol{x}_{1})

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{G}}\int_{\Omega^{d-d_{r}}}\tilde{K}_{l}((R_{1},\boldsymbol{x}_{1}),(R_{2},\boldsymbol{x}_{2}))g_{l}(R_{2},\boldsymbol{x}_{2})W_{d_{r},d}(\boldsymbol{x})\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\mathrm{d}R

\displaystyle=\sum_{m=1}^{N(l)}g_{lm}(R_{1},\boldsymbol{x}_{1})\langle g_{lm},g\rangle_{W_{d_{r},d}}=g_{l}(R_{1},\boldsymbol{x}_{1}),

using the orthonormality of
\{g_{lm}:l\in\mathbb{N},1\leq m\leq N(l)\}
in
\mathcal{L}_{2}(\mathbb{G}\times\Omega^{d-d_{r}},W_{d_{r},d})
.

Since
\mathbb{H}_{l}
’s are invariant subspaces with respect to the unitary representation
(\rho,\mathbb{H})
, it is trivial that
\tilde{K}_{l}((R,\boldsymbol{x}),(R,\boldsymbol{x}))
is invariant to the choice of
R\in\mathbb{G}
. Then,

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega^{d-d_{r}}}\tilde{K}_{l}((R,\boldsymbol{x}),(R,\boldsymbol{x}))W_{d_{r},d}(\boldsymbol{x})\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{G}}\int_{\Omega^{d-d_{r}}}\tilde{K}_{l}((R,\boldsymbol{x}),(R,\boldsymbol{x}))W_{d_{r},d}(\boldsymbol{x})\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\mathrm{d}R

\displaystyle=\sum_{m=1}^{N(l)}\int_{\mathbb{G}}\int_{\Omega^{d-d_{r}}}[g_{lm}(R,\boldsymbol{x})]^{2}W_{d_{r},d}(\boldsymbol{x})\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}\mathrm{d}R=N(l).

∎

C.3
Lemmas for Asymptotics

Example C.1
.

Consider the Radon transform with
d\geq 2
,
d_{r}=d-1
, and
W_{d_{r},d}(x)=w(x)^{-d_{r}}
, where
w(x)=\sqrt{1-x^{2}}
, along with a compatible kernel as described above.
For the Radon transform, it is more natural to index singular functions by a double array
(m,l)
and
k
, where
m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
l\leq m
and
l-m
is even, rather than using indices
l
and
m
. The singular functions are given by Zernike polynomials
[
64
,
70
]
:

\displaystyle g_{(m,l)k}(R,x)=c_{m}^{-1/2}Y_{lk}(R^{\top}e_{1})C_{m}^{d/2}(x)w^{d-1}(x),\quad k=1,\dots,N(l),

Here,
Y_{lk}:\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\to\mathbb{R}
are the spherical harmonics of degree
l
with the unit
\mathcal{L}_{2}
-norm,
C_{m}^{d/2}
is the Gegenbauer polynomial of degree
m
, and
c_{m}
is the normalization constant

c_{m}=\int_{-1}^{+1}(C_{m}^{d/2}(x))^{2}w^{d-1}(x)\mathrm{d}x=\frac{2^{d-1}\Gamma((d+1)/2)^{2}m!}{(m+d/2)\Gamma(m+d)},

The corresponding
(m,l)
-the kernel on the projection space
SO(d)\times[-1,+1]
is then:

\displaystyle\tilde{K}_{(m,l)}

\displaystyle((R_{1},x_{1}),(R_{2},x_{2}))

\displaystyle=\frac{N(l)}{c_{m}|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|}C_{l}^{d/2-1}(e_{1}^{\top}R_{1}R_{2}^{\top}e_{1})[C_{m}^{d/2}(x_{1})w^{d-1}(x_{1})][C_{m}^{d/2}(x_{2})w^{d-1}(x_{2})],

and satisfies (A), as proven in
Lemma

C.2
. In particular,
\tilde{B}=\pi^{-2}
when
d=2
.

Lemma C.2
.

Let
d\geq 2
,
m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
l\leq m
and
l-m
is an even integer. Then the reproducing kernel
\tilde{K}_{(m,l)}
defined in
Example

C.1
satisfy (A).
Moreover, in the case where
d=2
, we get the following sharp bound:

\displaystyle\sup_{(m,l),R,x}\frac{\tilde{K}_{(m,l)}((R,x),(R,x))}{N(l)}=\frac{1}{\pi^{2}}.

Proof.

Note that
\sum_{k=1}^{N(l)}Y_{lk}(\theta)^{2}=N(l)/|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|
for any
\theta\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}

[
17
]
, hence

\displaystyle\tilde{K}_{(m,l)}((R,x),(R,x))

\displaystyle=c_{m}^{-1}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{N(l)}Y_{lk}(R^{\top}e_{1})^{2}\right)[C_{m}^{d/2}(x)w^{d-1}(x)]^{2}

\displaystyle=c_{m}^{-1}N(l)[C_{m}^{d/2}(x)w^{d-1}(x)]^{2}/|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|.

Using Legendre’s Duplication formula
\Gamma((z+1)/2)\Gamma(z/2)=\sqrt{\pi}2^{1-z}\Gamma(z)
and (1.4) in
[
23
]
:

\displaystyle\sup_{|x|\leq 1}|C_{m}^{\lambda}(x)w^{2\lambda-1}(x)|\leq\frac{\Gamma(m/2+\lambda)}{\Gamma(\lambda)\Gamma(m/2+1)}\frac{\Gamma(2\lambda)m!}{\Gamma(m+2\lambda)},\quad\lambda\geq 1,

we obtain

\displaystyle\sup_{(m,l),R,x}\frac{\tilde{K}_{(m,l)}((R,x),(R,x))}{N(l)}

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\left(\frac{\Gamma((m+d)/2)}{\Gamma(d/2)\Gamma(m/2+1)}\frac{\Gamma(d)m!}{\Gamma(m+d)}\right)^{2}\frac{(m+d/2)\Gamma(m+d)}{2^{d-1}(\Gamma((d+1)/2)^{2}m!}\frac{\Gamma(d/2)}{2\pi^{d/2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\Gamma(d/2)}{2\pi^{d/2}}\frac{\Gamma(d)^{2}}{[\Gamma((d+1)/2)\Gamma(d/2)]^{2}2^{d-1}}\sup_{m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\left(\frac{\Gamma((m+d)/2)}{\Gamma(m/2+1)}\right)^{2}\frac{m!(m+d/2)}{\Gamma(m+d)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\Gamma(d/2)}{2\pi^{d/2}}\frac{2^{d-1}}{\pi}\sup_{m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\frac{(\Gamma((m+d)/2))^{2}}{\Gamma(m+d)}\frac{m!(m+d/2)}{(\Gamma(m/2+1))^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{2^{d-2}\Gamma(d/2)}{\pi^{d/2+1}}\sup_{m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\frac{\mathrm{Beta}((m+d)/2,(m+d)/2)}{\mathrm{Beta}(m/2+1,m/2+1)}\frac{m+d/2}{m+1},

which is finite.
In particular, when
d=2
,
C_{m}^{1}
is the Chebyshev polynomials of the second kind:

C_{m}^{1}(x)=\frac{\sin((m+1)\arccos x)}{(m+1)\sin(\arccos x)}=\frac{\sin((m+1)\arccos x)}{(m+1)w(x)},

and

\displaystyle\sup_{(m,l),R,x}\frac{\tilde{K}_{(m,l)}((R,x),(R,x))}{N(l)}

\displaystyle=\sup_{m,x}\frac{[\sin((m+1)\arccos x)]^{2}}{c_{m}(m+1)^{2}|\mathbb{S}^{1}|}=\sup_{m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\frac{1}{2\pi c_{m}(m+1)^{2}}

\displaystyle=\sup_{m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\frac{(m+1)\Gamma(m+2)}{4\pi(\Gamma(3/2))^{2}m!(m+1)^{2}}=\frac{1}{4\pi(\Gamma(3/2))^{2}}=\frac{1}{\pi^{2}}.

∎

Lemma C.3
.

Let
\mathscr{K}
be a compact, self-adjoint operator on a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
. Then, for any
\kappa>0
,
(\mathscr{K}+\nu I)^{-1}\mathscr{K}^{\kappa}
is also a compact, self-adjoint operator with the operator norm satisfying

\displaystyle\left\VERT(\mathscr{K}+\nu I)^{-1}\mathscr{K}^{\kappa}\right\VERT_{\infty}\leq\begin{cases}\kappa^{\kappa}(1-\kappa)^{1-\kappa}\nu^{-(1-\kappa)},\quad&0<\kappa<1,\\
\VERT\mathscr{K}\VERT_{\infty}^{\kappa-1},\quad&\kappa\geq 1.\end{cases}

When
\kappa=0
,
(\mathscr{K}+\nu I)^{-1}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})
is a self-adjoint operator with
\VERT(\mathscr{K}+\nu I)^{-1}\mathscr{K}^{\kappa}\VERT_{\infty}\leq\nu^{-1}
. Therefore, for a fixed
\kappa\geq 0
,
\VERT(\mathscr{K}+\nu I)^{-1}\mathscr{K}^{\kappa}\VERT_{\infty}\leq\VERT\mathscr{K}\VERT_{\infty}^{(\kappa-1)\vee 0}\nu^{(\kappa-1)\wedge 0}
.

Proof.

Let

\mathscr{K}=\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}\lambda_{l}e_{l}\otimes e_{l}

be the eigendecomposition of
\mathscr{K}
, where the eigenfunctions
\{e_{l}\}_{l=0}^{\infty}
is a CONS for
\mathcal{H}
with corresponding eigenvalues
\lambda_{1}\geq\lambda_{2}\geq\dots\geq 0
. Then for any
f=\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}f_{l}e_{l}\in\mathcal{H}
,

(\mathscr{K}+\nu I)^{-1}\mathscr{K}^{\kappa}f=\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}\frac{\lambda_{l}^{\kappa}}{\lambda_{l}+\nu}f_{l}e_{l}.

If
0<\kappa<1
, Hölder’s inequality yields

\displaystyle\lambda_{l}+\nu=\frac{(\lambda_{l}^{\kappa}\kappa^{\kappa})^{1/\kappa}}{1/\kappa}+\frac{(\nu^{(1-\kappa)}(1-\kappa)^{(1-\kappa)})^{1/(1-\kappa)}}{1/(1-\kappa)}\geq\kappa^{\kappa}(1-\kappa)^{(1-\kappa)}\lambda_{l}^{\kappa}\nu^{(1-\kappa)},

from which we obtain

\left\VERT(\mathscr{K}+\nu I)^{-1}\mathscr{K}^{\kappa}\right\VERT_{\infty}=\frac{\lambda_{1}^{\kappa}}{\lambda_{1}+\nu}\leq\kappa^{\kappa}(1-\kappa)^{1-\kappa}\nu^{-(1-\kappa)}.

On the other hand, if
\kappa=0
, then
1/(\lambda_{l}+\nu)\leq\nu^{-1}
, and if
\kappa\geq 1
, then

\frac{\lambda_{l}^{\kappa}}{\lambda_{l}+\nu}\leq\lambda_{l}^{\kappa-1}.

∎

For the covariance estimation, we recall that
J=(j,k)\,(1\leq j\leq r_{i},1\leq k\leq s_{ij})
denote a merged index.

Lemma C.4
.

Define
\boldsymbol{S}^{\otimes}:=\mathbb{E}[(\mathcal{Y}\otimes\mathcal{Y})\otimes_{2}(\mathcal{Y}\otimes\mathcal{Y})]\in\mathcal{B}_{1}(\mathbb{H}\otimes\mathbb{H})
, which is a trace-class operator with the norm

{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\boldsymbol{S}^{\otimes}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{1}=\mathbb{E}[\|\mathcal{Y}\otimes\mathcal{Y}\|_{\mathbb{H}\otimes\mathbb{H}}^{2}]=\mathbb{E}[\|\mathcal{Y}\|_{\mathbb{H}}^{4}]\leq M_{4}.

Then, for any
1\leq j_{1},j^{\prime}_{1},j_{2},j^{\prime}_{2}\leq r_{i}
with
j_{1}\neq j^{\prime}_{1},j_{2}\neq j^{\prime}_{2}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{E}\left[\left.Z_{iJ_{1}J^{\prime}_{1}}Z_{iJ_{2}J^{\prime}_{2}}\right|\varphi\right]\langle\varphi_{iJ_{1}J^{\prime}_{1}}^{\otimes},e^{\otimes}_{lm,l^{\prime}m^{\prime}}\rangle_{\mathbb{H}\otimes\mathbb{H}}\langle\varphi_{ij_{2}j^{\prime}_{2}}^{\otimes},e^{\otimes}_{lm,l^{\prime}m^{\prime}}\rangle_{\mathbb{H}\otimes\mathbb{H}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\begin{cases}\omega_{d_{r},d}^{-4}\langle\boldsymbol{S}^{\otimes}e^{\otimes}_{lm,l^{\prime}m^{\prime}},e^{\otimes}_{lm,l^{\prime}m^{\prime}}\rangle_{\mathbb{H}\otimes\mathbb{H}}\tau_{ll^{\prime}}^{4},\quad&|\{j_{1},j^{\prime}_{1}\}\cap\{j_{2},j^{\prime}_{2}\}|=0,\\
\omega_{d_{r},d}^{-2}(B^{4}{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\boldsymbol{S}^{\otimes}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{1}+2\sigma_{r_{i}}^{2}B^{2}{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\boldsymbol{\Gamma}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{\infty}+\sigma_{r_{i}}^{4})\tau_{ll^{\prime}}^{2},\quad&|\{j_{1},j^{\prime}_{1}\}\cap\{j_{2},j^{\prime}_{1}\}|\geq 1.\end{cases}

Proof.

Recall that for any
1\leq i\leq n
and
1\leq j\leq r_{i}
,
Z_{iJ}=\langle\mathcal{Y}_{i},\varphi_{iJ}\rangle_{\mathbb{H}}+\varepsilon_{iJ}
where
\varepsilon_{iJ}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle iid}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma_{r_{i}}^{2})
.

(1)

If
|\{j_{1},j^{\prime}_{1}\}\cap\{j_{2},j^{\prime}_{2}\}|=0
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left.Z_{iJ_{1}J^{\prime}_{1}}Z_{iJ_{2}J^{\prime}_{2}}\right|\varphi\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\left.Z_{iJ_{1}}Z_{iJ^{\prime}_{1}}Z_{iJ_{2}}Z_{iJ^{\prime}_{2}}\right|\varphi\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\left.\langle\mathcal{Y}_{i},\varphi_{iJ_{1}}\rangle_{\mathbb{H}}\langle\mathcal{Y}_{i},\varphi_{iJ^{\prime}_{1}}\rangle_{\mathbb{H}}\langle\mathcal{Y}_{i},\varphi_{iJ_{2}}\rangle_{\mathbb{H}}\langle\mathcal{Y}_{i},\varphi_{iJ^{\prime}_{2}}\rangle_{\mathbb{H}}\right|\varphi\right]=\langle\boldsymbol{S}^{\otimes}\varphi_{iJ_{1}J^{\prime}_{1}}^{\otimes},\varphi_{iJ_{2}J^{\prime}_{2}}^{\otimes}\rangle_{\mathbb{H}\otimes\mathbb{H}}.

Hence,
